3REAKFAST--SUPPEFI,

LADIES' LETTER. 1

MY DEAR NICLLIE, Already it may be said, that we have become' accustomed to the combination of fur and lace in millinery; fur and flowers mated together we know also; but fur and ostrich plumes are suffi- ciently original and effective to attract attention. A white velvet toque, very flat and wide, had a long white ostrich feather curled right round its brim, the quill part left visible, and apparently held in place by a broad strap of sable, whilst sable showed between the junction of quill and tip of the feather, and the head and paws of the little animal were poised at the side. Fur is used in Inillinery in quite a new manner this season. Instead of the heavy brim of fur, with velvet or *loth crown, of which practically everyone had Irearied, we find, perhaps, a soft crown of sable or breitschwanz, with rolled brim of tulle or lace or embroidery, tufts and rosettes of fur, or the head and paws arranged to fall over the brim at one side. The effect is lighter altogether.

The exceeding mildness of the weather admits the all-prevailing bolero being worn in the most becoming fashion, thrown open to display a pretty blouse and lace jabot, which are all the prettier by contrast with the handsome furs of collar and revers. This is smarter than the closed-up bolero, though not so practical in cold weather. The best dressed women are wearing the lightest and most dainty of blouses just now, regardless of the dam- aging effects of fog and damp; indeed, the skirt and blouse peeping from underneath a short well- cut bolero seem the favourite wear at present. Blouses of thick cream silk, with insertions and appliqu6 of gold lace and gold medallions, look particularly nice with a black skirt and bolero, and many have a sash of some vivid ribbon swathed round the waist and tied in a,' bow and ends at the side.

Evening gowns are to be of the softeat and most supple materials this winter, and stiff silks— except for trimming—muslins, and nets are no longer tolerated, as they will not lend themselves to the clinging style which is the mode. Among the cheaper stuffs, voile is still to the front, and very useful it is for wear at all functions except balls and large parties. A very nice evening dress suitable for Christmas festivities can be made of ivory voile. The skirt, which is slightly trained, has a couple of little frills at the hem, and is striped from the waist down, with bands of yellowish lace insertion. The bodice and sleeves are similarly striped, but the yoke, which is un- lined, is made of lace insertions arranged the oppo- site way, that is to say, running across the figure. The belt is of heliotrope antique satin with a hand- some paste buckle. This simple gown looks well carried out in black voile and lace insertions with a yoke of jetted lace and a deep belt of black glace dotted with jet stars. Another pretty white voile gown has an accordion-kilted skirt, and a full bodice with transparent lace yoke and sleeves, the cerselet belt being of soft white satin. A third dress of the same invaluable material has the skirt trimmed with an applique of ecru lace laid on so as to simulate a robing, a similar applique out- lining the yoke and bordering the short bell. sleeves. The yoke and under-sleeves are of white mousseline, and the belt is of gold galon.

Evening cloaks are various enough in kind and in character. Some are so,short as to barely pass the waist, a sort that is very unsuitable for winter wear. Others are long enough to trail several inches on the ground at the sides and back. The materials are of an infinite variety. The favourite for those who can command every luxury is fur. The sable coat is long and full, with rather wide sleeves. The ermine coat flows out as amply as a tea-gown, and is as costly as it is easily solh.d. Then oome the chiffon coats, all frills and tucks, with a warm lining of quilted satin or brocade. Black satin follows on, also warmly quilted under- neath, and usually richly finished with lace about the neck and shoulders. Fancy materials, usually broch6 or stamped or crushed velvet, come before the cloth coats. It is made of pink cloth and trimmed with icru lace and black velvet. The lace yoke and collar are bordered with frills of the pink cloth, and the sleeves, long and loose, are finished with frills and la-3e. Cashmere and kin- dred textures are also used for making these long cloaks, some of which are lined with brightly- tinted faille or veiling, others with silk or woollen brouche. Many are furnished with fur collars, and a few are lined throughout with fur. An ideal coat is black silk of the gros-grain type, lined with dark mink, and with a collar to match; but this is very costly, and, besides the dark fur, is almost always worn outside this winter. Your loving,

WOMEN'S CHAI.

December is a month which has mixed memories for the Royal Family. The first is the Queen's birthday, and it hardly seems possible that her Majesty has entered upon her fifty-eighth Iyear. Forty years ago, cu the 14th December Prince Consort died, and on the same day in 1878, the death of Princess Alice took place, at Darmstadt. It was also in December in 1871 that his Majesty then Prince of Wales, was almost at death's door, during hie serious illness from typhoid fever, and just ten years ago, on December 23rd. it was announced that Prince George, now Prince of Wales, was happily recovering from a similar illness. The fourteenth day of the month,ia generally regarded as an unlucky day for Royalty, and this is especially the case when the 14th December falls, as it does this year, on a Saturday. George I. George 11. George 111. George IV. and William IV., all died on a Saturday, and our Royal Family of to-day have especial cause to regard the anniversary cf Prince Consort's death as a day of sadness and ill omen. The fourteenth day of the month seems also to have been made memorable by the assassins. Orsini, Charlotte Corday, Ravaillac, all made tbeir murderous attacks on the 14th and it was on the same day in April 1865, that Pfesident Lincoln was shot. The matrimonial troubles between the Grand Duke and Grated Duchess of Besse, seem likely to end in a similar dissolution of marriage, as that last year of the separation between Princess Louise of Schleswig- Holstein, and Peirce Aribert of Anbalt. These family differetces amongst her grandchildren were a great source of trouble to our late Queen Victoria, who, on more than ene occasion smoothed things over for the Grand Ducal couple of Besse. , The Grand Duke, who has just turned thirty three, is the only surviving son of the late Princess Alice, and the Duchess was Princess Victoria Melita. the second daugh- ter of the late Duke of Saxe-Coburg-Gotha. Theirßoyal Highnesses are consequently much more nearly related to King Edward than to the German Emperor, but it would seem that all idea gf effecting a reconciliation between the Duke and Duchels has been given up, and, as the Grand Duke is an Independent Sovereign in his own domain, it is not a matter in which either the Royal Families of Great Britain or Germany can inter- fere. The orange blossom would not appear to play so im- portant a part in the fashionable bride's toilettee, as it was once wont to do. The reason given for this state of affairs, is, that the heavy perfume of the real flower causes a feeling of faintness to the wearer, and the arti- ficial specimen cannot, of course, be tolerated for a moment. One distinguished bride went to the altar , the otber day wearing a wreath of white clematis, while another appeared in one of white heather, and yet another in a wreath of white heather and myrtle— mixed. Undoubtedly the desire to strike a note of originality is the real cause of this change in ideas, but be this as it may little is lost by it, and possibly some- thing is gained, from an artistic poitt of view. There seems to be no prevailing fashion in evening wraps. 1 hers are the small capes, which are draughty, and very unsuitable for the present season, the little coats which are distinctly more desirable, and best of all the long cosy wraps which envelop one, and trail some inches on the ground. The fabrics of which these garments may be built are legion. Silk of the yro: grain type is a favourite one, at the moment,so is satin, then there are the crushed or stamped velvets, chiffon, cloth and caehmere, not to mention numerous fancy materials. In the way of linings, fur plays an impor- tant part, next on the list comes quilted satin. while very thick fabrics show a simple lining of veiling. or silk brochii. Fur and lace appear about the neck and 1 shoulders of all evening wraps, forming a becoming and delightful finish. The pa lest shades, and white, continue to be the smartest wear for walking and dressy afternoon toilettes, while for evening gowns colours are as delicate and dreamy-looking as the materials themselves. This effect is often brought about by three or four tints being employed, one over another, when the Wearer would appear to be imprisoned in a soft fleecy cloud. Sleeves (?) for full dress toilettes, have, it is amusing to notice, undergone a slight change since vaccination became the order of the day. Fermerly the most slender of straps across the shoulders, were deemed sufficient, now these are supplemented by others droop log upon the arm, and so arranged as to conceal the three familiar, but unbecoming red mark!. The prettiest children's parties are those which the little guests attend all in white or in fancy dress, and such tunctions promise to be fairly numerous this Christmae-tide. One reason for this extra gaiety pos- sibly lies in the fact, that with so many of one men fclk still at the front, the desire for great balls is checked. The little folke however, cannot be allowed to miss their pleasuree,and their parties are often made an excuse for bestowing some pleasure upon grown up belongings. So their particular entertainments are somewhat numerous, much doubtless, to their satis- faction. When fancy dress is the order of the day, all the boys want to be soldiers of course. These are usually of the brightest and most gorgeousidescription, dust-coloured warriors being few and far between. For girls, no dresses are prettier than flowers, fresh childish faces blending so well with the idea. Some time ago, a widespread notion got about that cancer was caused by eating tomatoes, and now the idea hss been revived that it can be cured by an infusion of violet leaves. In spite of the disbelief of the medical journals, in tbia theory, it is a fact that violets have a decided effect upon the voice, and among other cases one is recorded of a celebrated vocalist, who was unable to sing after inhaling the perfume of a bouquet largely composed of violets. It has also been observed in music classes, that people wearing a bunch of violets, were not in good voice, and the great vocalist Faure, in his book on the voice, says, that the three things to be avoided by the singer, are alcohol, tobacco, and violets. It IS therefore quite easy to understand how the old super- stition arose that violets were a cure for cancer, and it is quite possible that the medical journals may be pre- mature in dismissing the idea that they possess curative properties. Speaking of vocalists, and voices, reminds one of songs, an especially important subject at this season of the year. Like new novels, new songs are plentiful, so I plentiful that the task of selection is a difficult one, bet urquestionably there is much rubbish on the market.at the present time, and much that is merely passable. But it is equally true that there is much that is really good, quaint, and out of the beaten track. Under the last category may be mentioned :—" Three Songs by Edna Rosalind Park,(Enoch and Sons). No 1, " A Thought" No. 2, "A Melody," No. 3, "Love." This collection will be much appreciated by all in search of pretty, yet simple ballade. Another delightful song, or rather song- cycle, published by the same firm is " Summer-time," No. 1, " Daybreak," No. 2, " Morning," N 0.3, " Even- ing," Ne. 4, " Night," with 'cello obbligato, a charming collection, whicb should be secured by all vocaliste. That we all require something to do, to keep us out of mischief, is perhaps nowhere better illustrated than in the growing practice of smoking amongst the wealthy and leisured class of women: No one ever sees or thinks of a woman of the busy middle class smoking, The very idea seems to suggest an absurdity, but, any afternoon one may see women smoking at the so-called high class and billiard m who have nothing lAatdsieesv'ecrlaulbost these just as shment aessutanbctoincernedsl,y as if they were men. smoking men's rooms caitirebjsu. and a s ammuocnhe thesten w women thing, as at the better to do the cult of the tobacco habit, seems to come as easy and natural as if they were born to it. A QUEEN'S SOLDIER.

BY WALTER WOOD, Arathor of "Barrack and Battlefield," "Famous British Warships," "Through Battle to Pro- motion," "A Captain in the Postilion," "A Soldier of the Legion," &c.

CHAPTER XVII. SHELVED.

Mark by this time had seen enough of war to know that a hurried retreat was being made, that the British must be very near, and that nothing in the nature of a stand was to be offered to their advance. He lay flat upon the ground, no longer thinking of his weariness, his hunger or his thirst; seeing only the figures in the station lights, and hearing nothing but the clamour of voices and the confused noises of the entraining. At last, and Mark knew that it must be very near the dawn, the train moved away and rumbled past him, then stopped. 'He was wondering why this should be when he heard a succession of explosions, and looking towards the flashes he saw that they came from the track itself. Their meaning was clear, the Boers were destroying the permanent way; and he judged that they con- sidered their case desperate. Mark heard cries of triumph—the shouts of the enemy at the success of their explosions—and then the train re-started. This time there was no more stopping, and when the day broke Mark saw that he was alone, and that Boota's farm was almost within hail, rising grey and lonely from the veld. There was something 'unspeakably sad in the appearance of the homestead, and the trooper found himself almost unconsciously picturinr, some such scene of desolation as this in which the main object was the Hill Top Farm and the chief sufferers were his parents. He assurned, from the look of the buildings and the land about them, that no one had been left at the farm, and that Mrs. Toota, having stuck to her home and her post to the very last, had withdrawn with her people. "If that's so," muttered Mark, "it's queer that they've left the place standing instead of firing it or blowing it up. But I suppose they were much too busy and in too big 's hurry to bother with a trifle like that. Anyway, I most go and try my luck." He set his 'face towards the buildinp, ,and presented himself at the door out of which he had been carried to the station as a prisoner of %var. It was so long before ,an answer was made that he began to think that he was right in assuming that the farm was entirely deserted ; but -just as he was wondering how best he could enter the building the door was opened slowly and Mrs. Boota• con- fronted him.

She made no remark and shewed no surprise. Outwardly, there was nothing to shew that she was one of the -victims of war. So far as he could judge she was just the same placid woman that he had noticed when he had been taken to the farm a wounded man.

For some seconds Mark remained speechless. Then he said: "Don't you know me, missis ?" "No," was the answer. "What do you want? Who are you? I thought eve*, man had gone off by the train." "I'm Mark Berm. I was here weeks since, with a damaged leg," said the trooper. "It's damaged still—and worse. But it has good reason to be." "You'ie a sorry-looking man," said Mrs. Boots. "I couldn't tell who it was. You're changed." She looked at his bearded, haggard face, and his miserable ragged clothes. She looked, too, at his bare, bruised feet, and her eyes grew dim. She had seen Much of war's horrors by this time, but no such pleading and pathetic sight as Mark made at that open door in the perfect stillness of the early morn. "Changed I" said Mark. "I've been lost on the veld for a week, missis, and had thrown myself down, praying to God to get it over soon, when I found that I was near the railway. I got on to a train and slipped out when it stopped at the station there. I ran away from prison, but I didn't know what there was at the end of it." "But why did you escape? They would have looked after you there," said Mrs. Boota, leading the way into the r6om which Mark had seen before. He recognised the furniture, but saw that it had been roughly handled anti.disavvonged. The walls, too, were battered and broken in places, as if shots had been fired upon them wantonly. In one place there was a large jagged hole, through which the sky could be seen, a hole that could have been made only by a field-gun or larger weapon. "It was for the sake of a woman," said Mark. "She's here, in this country, and I want to see her. That's the real reason why I broke adrift." "Ah l" said Mrs. Boota. "Take that chair and I'll give you some coffee and bread and meat. I'd give you more, but they've taken everything they could lay their hands on. You see what they've done to the house, too." "Have they got here?" asked Mark, eagerly. "I don't mean your people, I mean mine," said the woman, bitterly. "They took my' husband ,and son first; now there isn't a living creature on the farm but me. They wanted me to go with them, saying that the English were not ten...miles off, but I told them I'd stay. The English couldn't be'worse to me than some of my own people have been. Some of them suspect us, and one or two who hate us came .in one day and knocked the things about, as you see. They seemed to think it fun to fire at the walls from the doorway and the open window. One of the bullet's, though, came back, and hurt the man who'd fired it.."

WI 118.

1 pt.trti

it didn't kill him, missis," growled Mark. "But what about the big hole up there ?" "That was done by some of your people, who came up suddenly one day with their guns and were off again almost before we knew they'd been. But never mind about the farm. All this is old. Did you see anybody in Pretoria?" "I saw your son, Mrs. Boota, and gave him your message," said Mark, hastily, and with the sole desire to offer consolation. This woman was of the same nature as himself, no wearer of the heart upon the sleeve, no glib confider of her woes and wrongs. She suffered in silence and sought, no sympathy, and a slack observer might have thought she took her troubles easily. Butll/ii.rk understood what she was passing through when he saw that since he was last at the farm Mrs. Boota's black hair had become tinged with grey, and her smooth brow was crossed with lines. "He's well and strong, and so is his father. There's a knife your son gave me. It saved my life ;in fact, if ever I get back to England I shall never forget that I'm Living just because I came across you and your son." .Mark held out the knife as he spoke so that she might see it. "Not all that," said the farmer's wife, who had quickly become animated, "not all that; but, thank God, there's some bright spot in all this fearful business. When you've eaten and drunk you shall tell me all about my son and his father. But not now. I can wait for news better thaa you can wait for food." Mark, without more words, took his simple break- fast. It seemed as if he could never have enough, but he was satisfied at last. He was still hungry, but he knew how his companion yearned for news of her absent ones. Ile told all he knew Of the son and all he had heard of the father. When he had finished the woman rose and said:

-^7I think if God meant this war to come upon us and make the misery it has done, He intant to soften things down a bit, and so ptit you in the way of coming here and coming back. • I'm thanki ful for it. To talk with one who's seen my son and his father is the next best thing to seeing them myself. Though I believe that will be soon. Don't you see how we're getting driven in? That commando you saw was driven back by English soldiers last week and settled here. Now it's been driven back further, and we shall see your own people here before the day is out. There was a queer-looking train ran up nearly to the station yesterday. It • was a train and yet not a train. You could see nothing but straight sides with rifles popping out." "Alll An armoured train," said Mark. "What happened ? " "It came on as far as the curve over there and then the guns here tried to smash it. There was a surprise on both sides. Your people didn't expect to find a commando here, and ours didp't expect to see the train come round the corner." d' And what about the train ? " asked Benn, deeply interested. "It got away. The men blazed hard with their rifles, and one of the cgins hero was hurried up to try and destroy it, but the train was too quick. The engine backed it out of danger, and it wasn't so much as struck." "And how far off do you say our people are ? " asked Mark, delighted with the news. "When the train had gonea few of the men from here rode off to find out. They Caine back and said tt t_ktra was a big British force not ten mil

sway, and that it held the railway, Then the trait was sent for, and they all got into it in the night. Most likely you'll see your own folk before the day's out.' "I shall thank God if I do," said Mark, fervently. "I should reckon my week on the veld as nothing then." „-_

"If they come they'll find nothing here but wreckage," said the woman. "We've been thirty pars in getting this farm together. When the war's over we shall have to start afresh at the beginning. It'll be a terrible task. I feel as if I should neves have the heart to do it." "Butisome of the farmers as well as the farms have gone," said Mark, offering what he knew to be lame comfort; but it was the best he could do. "I heard in Pretoria of a man and three sons being killed in one fight, and his farm smashed up and burnt on top of that. You'll find that our people won't ton& a stick, and as for your son and his father I do believe they'll.soon come back again. The war will soon finish:"

"I don't care how, so long as they come back," said Mrs. Boota. "But lay down on the couch and sleep. Don't say you won't, because you can't keep your eyes open. Besides, your leg wants resting. Let me do it up for you again." Mark, 'with abrupt expressions of gratitude, took the couch, and the farmer's wife washed and bandaged his leg. The privations of the week had made the wound,much worse, and now that there was no need for more enforced exertion and end to his 'perils, Benn speedily became helpless. The pain of the leg was intense, every bone and muscle in his body seemed to ache, and physically the trooper was utterly exhausted. He tried to sleep, but his efforts failed; and he was content to watch the silent woman of sorr6vv into whose quiet life war had brought such devastation. Everything was very still, very peaceful, where so lately all had been noise and excitement, and, despite his suffering's, Mark fell into an untroubled sleep.

CHAPTER XVIII. A FRIENDLY OFFICER.

For several hours he was oblivious to all that passed. When at last he did wake up, he saw three khaki-clad men, with rifles and fixed bayonets, standing by the couch. One was prodding him rather roughly with the butt, and the other two were shaking him and ordering him to get up and plant himself in the Middle of the floor. All the while they talked energetically, and looked as if they were enjoying themselves. "An' this is really one of 'em, is it ?" said the man who was prodding. "Well, he's the first I've seen so near; but, by George! if they're all as dirty, I'll believe what I've heard said of the Boers—that some of 'em don't know soap when they see it. Come, Mr. Piggy, wake up an' fall in. This dodge isn't good enough: You can't deceive us, you know—you're a prisoner, and you've got to come with us to the Gen'ral." "Gen'ral ? " repeated Mark, rising to a sitting posture. "Is there one here? What's his name, and what sort of people has he got with him? Are they all infantry, or is it a mixed lot ? " "Hang it!" exclaimed the soldier, thumping his rifle on the floor of the room, "this beats me; this chap talks English as well as I do." "I ought to," answered Mark, "seeing that I'm as much an Englishman as you are." "English!" said the soldier. "Then, by George! the Gen'ral 'll sling you up to the first tree. You know, there are two or three o' your sort doin' lot o' mischief. Come on; I wouldn't be in your shoes for a quart o' beer—an' that's sayin' a lot, with a thirst like mine."

"And not only English," added Mark, "but a soldier, too." "Which ridgiment ? " asked the private, hope- lessly bewildered. "Ninetieth Company, Imperial Yeomanry." "Oh A noodle," remarked the soldier, tolerantly, "Well, you don't look it. If you're one o' them, how is it you're 'ere so soon before the rest? They're only just landin' ; an' the raissis at this place—she speaks English too—says you've been 'ere' for hours. I don't like the look of it, eh, boys?" The comrades admitted that they didn't like the look of it, either. They were rather disappointed to have a prisoner torn from them in this way. "The comp'ny's here, do you say?" demanded Bann. "How's the Major? Old Tally-ho we call him. And do you know the horse he's riding?" "Weil, I don't know your officer," rejoined the private, beginning to have faith in Mark. "But if 'e's a tough old chap who yells 'untin' calls when 'S's goin' for 'em, an' rides the best horse in these parts, I suppose it's the same man. 'E does sort o' answer to your nickname." "Black, glossy horse, with a white star between the eyes," continued Benn. "Plenty_ of action about him, and as fit as a fiddle." "Well, now," replied the soldier, "you're sort o' court-martialling me, an' I don't like it. It reminds me too much of the past. But as for the horse, it seems to be the spme. They say the old chap stole it, or something, or wanted to steal it, an' so sent the real owner out to get shot. That's as I've 'eard the story, though maybe it's a bit twisted. But, 'owever that may be, there seems to be no question that, it's a good gee-gee." . . . _

"That llorse is mine," vowe'd Ben, eergetically. "I'm the man that brought Min up. I'll stagger that old Major a bit; don't you say anything." "But you'll have to come," said the soldier. "You know, we can.'t give you authority to be at. Jumpson to yer pins, an' we'll parade out before the Colonel ; then the General'll tackle you. I fancy there'll be a bit of sport to be got out o' you, judgin', from your looks. "How can I jump, with a leg like this?" said Mark, pointing to his wounded limb. "I couldn't stand now if you were to shoot me if I didn't. As for sport, I don't think I've had as much fun as you seem to fancy." "Look here," said the soldier, who had taken upon himself the conduct of the examination. "In a few words, let's have your hist'ry." Very briefly, Mark related his adventures, from the time of his capture till the beginning of that very day. The privates listened as closely as if they had been hearing a romance or enjoying an enthralling play. "Well, it sounds all right, eh, boys ?" said the soldier. "There's one thing certain, if he can't stand, he can't bolt, supposin' he shouldn't 'appen after all to speak the truth. Look here, Simpson, you go an' fetch the Captain. I'll wait here, an' see 'e doesn't slope." "You go an' fetch the Captain yourself," said Simpson. "I'm not goin' to take orders from you." The private used stroll,"' language, but as Simpson remained obdurate, and the other soldiers sided with him, the leader had to go and fetch the Captain himself. Before he went, he expressed the hope that the next engagement would remove a few soldiers whose names he did not deem it necessary to specifi, but who, he was well assured, could very easily be spared by the regiment. But the Captain was nowhere near. He was hurrying about seeing to his company, and wondering where half his men had got to. Failing him, and seeing the man whom he suppposed to be the officer commanding the Ninetieth' Company of the Imperial Yeomanry, he approached and 'said that lt man whit. called himself Benn was lying wounded in the farm kitchen.

The Major, astonished and delighted, hurried to Mark, and recognised him instantly. "I'm glad to see, you back, my lad," he exclaimed. "You shall tell me all about it in a few minutes. I've such a lot to do, I hardly knOw how to get through it. But I don't know; perhaps the best way will be to give you two or three minutes now, just sd that I can hear what you've got to say. Now, Benn, fire ahead. I'll light my pipe and smoke while you talk." "I say, sir," said Mark. "How's Prince? I shall be ready for him in a few days." He sprang the question upon his superior officer without the slightest warning. The Major was dumbfounded. Prince was the pride of his heart; to ride him had been to enable the Major to scoff at less favoured persons, and to have him torn away now was more than he could endure to think of. To be deprived of his treasure in this outrageous fashion would be to expose himself to merciless twittings from his friends. , "Prince is fit, my lad," he said, pulling his beard. "But you're in no condition for riding. I can see that with half an eye, and without asking questions." "I soon shall be, sir," protested Mark. "Not with a leg like that," said the 3fairie, casting a critical eye upon the swollen limb. "You'll have to go down to the base hospital. Look here, don't you know Sister Lizzie, as they call her ? "

"What aboufher ?" asked Mark, hoarsely. "If you know anything, sir, do tell me. I'm just sick to have some news. From the time I left her at Capetown to the time of coining here I've not had so much as a line from her—although, of course, I know that a man can't expect to get letters here as he gets 'ern at home." "Well, she's at the base—and a ripper she is, too. Would you rather have Prince or see the rather see Lis. sir." said Beam r 2,12.;

" Very good," said the crafty Major. "111 lee' tliat you get through to the right quarter. Now, take my word for it, you'll be no further use airing this war. That needn't bother you—you've done your share. As for Prince, I'll see that he's looked after as well as he has been up to the present. When the trouble's ended he shall ba given back to you. Will that do?" "It'll suit me, sir," said Mark. "Right; then I'll see that you join Sister Lizzie —is it your sister, or the missis—to—be ?—befora you're a day older." The Major looked slyly at Mark as he spoke, about Lizzie, but kindly, too, for he understood the position, and was interested in his hardy trooper. He was immensely proud of Mark's achievement in escaping and of his endurance on the veld, for he demanded, and got, a résumé of his adventures. He was gratified, too, that one. of his men should be the means of bringing to his own people a good deal of information relating to the enemy; for a few questions shewed that Mark had not forgotten to make the best use of his opportunities at Pretoria and at the wayside place whence the Boers had retreated so hurriedly in the early morning. It was not a great deal that the trooper had been able to tell, but -what mostly delighted the Major was that he should have put into practice several simple principles which he lad never tired of impressing upon his men since they came out on active service. Apart from his wish to remove from his imme— diate neighbourhood such a dangerous competitor for Prince as Mark, the Major was prompted by a- desire to do the best he could for one of his people who had shed such lustre upon the Yeomanry, and he had been keenly delighted to find that Benn was \ so deeply interested in Lizzie as he had just proved himself to be. _ _ .

_ The acquaintance of the young lady herself the Major had made while on a brief visit to the Yeomanry hospital to see how some of his troopers' fared. Lizzie's energy and ability had claimed his' attention, and ,eyond that he had been interested by the statement which Donald had volunteered that she was engaged to be married to one of hie own troopers. He4had little trouble in satisfying himself as to the trooper's identity. Now that he had so unexpectedly come across Mark ar,ain, and had made his peace with him concerning Prince— a very vital matter, in the Major's estimation—he was moved to tell him all that he could remember of the sayings and doings of Lizzie. He waxed romantic concerning her. According to him, never was there such a wonder in the field of nurses as she was. "Certainly," said the Major, "a there are lots of men of good standing who'd be very glad if they could get the chance to marry her and settle down here. I suppose you haven't any wish to do that yourself, Benn ? " ri "No, thank you, sir," answered Mark, with respectful firmness. "I'm too fond of the old country. Besides, I've got too many interests there.

"Ah !" said the Major. He was a very poor man himself, and it struck him as being somewhat odd that a mere trooper should be talking quietly of his interests—obviously financial—in England. The Major was an old cavalry officer, accustomed to the old way of doing,things, and not yet quite comprehending that there were amongst the rank and file of the Yeomen those who were far better circumstanced than himself.

"Of course, in that case, "he said, "I can understand that you won't want to stay here longer than you can help. If I were in your shoes, I should be wanting to get back, too. Your father's a farmer, isn't he ?" "Yes, sir," said Mark. i ~ "Does he rent the land, or own it?' continued the Major, who was by nature inquisitive, especially ~ on any point regarding the incomes of people connected in any way with the land. He saw no. impropriety in thus directly questioning Mark. "Owns it, lock, stock, and barrel, sir,' replied the trooper, delighted to talk on such a deeply attractive subject. "Yon see, it's been in the family for several generations and it's grown a lot - in value even in my time., I've heard father say that it must be worth five thousand pounds more than it was when he was a lad. It's because it's so much nearer to the big towns, and some of it would make such good building land. But the old man won't sell for building, any more than I shall, unless I'm forced. Then you know, there's Lizzie's father's place. That must be worth as much as ours, nearly. When we get married it'll be a case of joining up the two properties, in a way." "Why," exclaimed the Major, "you're positively s, bloated land-owner 1" "No, I', 01- 1

Mark: two lots must bd

lut what do-you sup, worth?" queried the Major. "Well, that I can't tell you, off-hand, sir, buff if you say that ours is worth thirty thousand an Jackson's twenty, you make fifty. I.should think that's well within the limit."

"Fifty thousand pounds!" exclaimed the Major. "Why, that's quite a nice tidy fortune, even for a gentleman like me. And yet I've hardly as many thousand shillings."

"Perhaps you; people had a way of getting through yours, sir," suggested Mark. "Of course," returned the Major, "what else should they do? The property was there, anct money was made to Spend, wasn't it?" "Yes," agreed Mark ; "but, all the same, there's been'a way in our family of both spending and: keeping it., ' "Then all I can say about your family is that they've been very wonderftil people," said ths Major, who did-not understand how such human, freaks came into existence.

"No, there's nothing queer about it," continued' Mark. "The thing's been simple enough. The sons, have gone on these lines—that what's been good' enough for the fathers has been good enough for them. _Besides, they've done what I've heard men speak about since I came here—sat tight." "And you'll do the same, I suppose?" said the Major. "I shall do the same, sir," answered Mark, with placid conviction. "It's in the breed." "And what you've seen away from home hasn't' made you dissatisfied ?" "Not a bit, sir; in fact, it's made me morev satisfied than ever I might have been if I'd gone' on in the old way. I got restless at times, and. wanted to go about and see things. I've done it, and I shall be glad to go bAk and be satisfied." The Major rose to take his leave. "Well, my Lad," he said, "I wish you luck enough to get back safely with your sweetheart. You'll see what she' can do for you when you get to the hospital. Good-bye." He shook hands before he left.

Mark was somewhat surprised, because, excellent though he was in many ways, the officer was some— what unpacious in his dealings with his men. But the fact was he had been greatly impressed during tho last few minutes. He was only human, and he looked more favourably upon a man whour he now knew to be well-to-do, if not actually rich, lian he had regarded him when he took him to be, mere trooper, a respectable Yeoman, like the, rest.

SIR J. T. BRUNNER, ON IHE ISFUTURE OF .ENGLISH GRAMMAR

SCHOOLS.

Sir J. T. Brunner, M.P., was the principal guest at the annual dinner of the Old Wittoniance. Society—a society of Old Northwich Grammar School boys—on Monday evening. In the course of an interesting speech he pointed out that the result of the Charity Commis- sioners' action in turning nearly all village grammar schools into boarding schools had been to excluc,e the boys of the villages and to put them into a vastly interior social position, so that the school, far from being a source of light and leading, became a source of social degradation. The influence of the csainty council was coming as a cure for this mis- chievous tendency, and more and more the brightest boys all over the country were being brought under the hospitable roofs of the grammar schools, and once again, through the action of the Technical Instruction Act and the scholarships given by the county councils, the schools were becoming, as they were intended to be by their pious founders, a source of mental improve- ment to the villages: He was satisfied that the Scotch habit of all classes going to school together was the cause of Scotchmen standing by each other so heartily, and for that reason he believed it was that they took so large a tMare in the government of the worid. They were looking forward a few months hence to legislation 6 which Miyht touch the grammar schools of England and Wales, and already he was glad to see that the Cheshire , Council had decided that an inquiry should be made into the circumstances of every grammar .school in Cheshire. Many of them were practically lost, but he hoped that the great interest which was being shown would have the effect of establishing a closer connection between such institutions and the local authsrities.— (Applause).

MANCHESTER MATTERS.

A Commotion by Belle Vue Gardens.

A commotion caused recently in close proximity to Belle Vue Gardens is likely to attain more than local notoriety. Not far from the Longsight entrance is a new street named Stainer-street. Some of the housis are still in course of erection. Among those already occupied, which face the open fields, but are still unnumbered, is what hereafter will be known as " No. 10." It is the home of Mr and Mrs Dempsey, who have only been married three years,and have one child. In the course of that comparatively brief period Mrs Dempsey has experienced a phase of life which is as interesting as it is remarkable. It is the accounts of this experience which have spread in the neighbourhood which have caused the commotion referred to. The most authentic account of the matter is that given by Mrs Dempsey to a Manchester reporter :

===== 0002601_19011206_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
[Rs DEMPSEY.

[From a Photograph by Charles Duval Oldham-street, _Manchester.) "1 have been married three years," she said, 'I and about two years ago I became very ill. I did not know what was the matter with me, but I got very weak and thin. I lost my appetite altogether, and Could scarcely walk. I tried various remedies recom- mended to me by friends, and then I went to a doctor. Be examined me, and told me I was suffering from aummia. This was quite evident,as I had become very , pals and bloodless, and the slightest exertion exhausted me. My appetite was very poor, and I did not seem to I obtain any nourishment from the little I did eat." " Did the doctor prescribe for you ? " " Yes, I was under his treatment for seven months. I took one bottle of medicine every week during those seven months, besides two boxes of pills. I did not seem to be getting any better ; in fact, I thought that I was worse, so I went to another doctor, and I had one bottle of medicine from him, but I failed to notice any benefit. lam twenty-four years of age, so you can judge that I was in an unenviable plight for a woman of my age. I bad been suffering about four months b€- fore I went to a doctor at all, but by this time I was to bad that I often had to remain in bed during the entire day. It was after I cothrulted the second doctor, one day when I was feeling a little better, that I I happened to be in Attrincham, and met a young lady named Jessie Shaw. She naturally asked me about my illness and I told her what I hare already told you. She then said * If I were you I would try Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, for they cured me ' I deter- mined to give Dr. Williams' pink pills a trial, and they have cured mess well." "I see you are quite well, but I want you to tell me exactly the effect Dr. Williams' pink pills had upon you, and how many boxes you took altogether." " I went to Mr. Brooks', chemist, flyde-road, and bought the first box ; in fact, I have bought all of them there, and I have only taken four boxes altogether. I felt somewhat better after I had taken tbe first box. I continued taking the pills, closely following the instructions, and the improvement was becoming more marked all the time. I began to feel stronger ; the languidness seemed to be desetting me, and I felt stronger and more cheerful. My friends noticed that I was acquiring a better colour. I slept better, my appe- tite gradually returned, and by the time I had finished the fourth box I was as well and hearty as you see me now." _ _

" It is astonishing what Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people have done in many cases that I have seen, Do you know of anyone elee that bas received benefit from them ? " " Yes, Miss Shaw, of whom I have eroken,and I have heard of other eases." " May I make this story of yours publie,and publish your photograph ?" ":3ertainly. You can have it with pleasure. I will sit for it n(xt week." Mrs Dempsey's younger sister was present during the interview, and effusively confirmed, at every stage, tbo story told.

The Police Court and Prison Gate Mission. The following is a summary of the principal work of the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission of the Liverpool Diocesan Church of England Temperance Society for the past month. Twenty six police and petty sessional Courts have been attended, and during 261 visits 1150 persons were conversed with of whom 716 were subseluebtly visited in their abodes, 84 women, girls and boys were taken to homes provided with employment or restored to friends. Employment was offered at the Society's. firewood and mat factory to 32 men and 11 others were provided with other work In 45 other instances the loss of employment was averted by the efforts of the missionaries. Clothing was furnished in 50 instances to enable persons to accept work. In 77 instances the magistrates asked ; for and obtained the help of the missionaries on behalf of persons charged. The police also had their assistance on behalf of 80 persons. The names and addresses of 51 persons were sent to ministers of religion. No less than 690 persons were provided with breakfast, advice, work &c., at 11 daily openings of the Mission room rear Walton Prison. 69 persons were visited in their abodes from the prisons, 61 persons were spoken to on cart stands and shelters, and 126 others at the railway stations. 129 persons were also spoken to in lodging-houses, 51 meetings were addressed. 203 pledges were taken &c. A sum of .E 7 Ils has been received from the members of the Grand Jury at the present Liverpool .Assizes being half of the " fines:' This is the third occasion that help has been received from this source.

Knutsford-road could not be said to be a suburban district. The whole of it was within the borough, and/ fully half or three.quarters of the pa Bangers carried were working men. and their wives and families. What about Liverpool-road? That could not be considered suburban, whatever mielat be said about Kautsford-road.—(l voice "It is highly re- spectable." and laughter.) It was highly respectable, but it was a purely workieg-class district, and War- riogton was a working-class town. Wherever they took the trams the) were bound to consider the interests of the working-class. They all knew that the Wilderspool-road section had not been a complete piece of work, and as a matter of pure business they wanted to tap the districts on the other side. He hoped the Corporation would not allow anyone else to take the powers. They had had many obj Pct lessons of people from outside taking their monopolies for which they (the Corporation) had eventually to pay for through the nose. With reference to the resolution relating to the altera- tion of the buildings of the old gas works, he wished to state that it only meant making goo 1 a certain portion of the present offices which were cut off at the corner to enable them to est in with a wider entrance to the proposed car sheds. replyln to C onecillor SMETHURST, The DEPUTY MAYOR said it was never intended to build new car sheds there. The committee were busi- ness men,and he thought they had regard for the town's finance, although at tee present time they were not sup- posed to have. (Laughter.) The people who complained about the rates were the people who were raising all the difficulties, and wished to rush them into all kinds of expense. They were not going to be so rushed. He assured them they had not lost their heads if people outside had done SO. (Laughter.) It never was intended to spend this sum of money included in the estimates for providing a new car shed if the present buildings at the gas works csuld be adapted. They had been found to be adaptable, and they were well within the Parlia- mentary estimate of the cost of tne tramways. Councilor SMETHURST replied that the plans of the car sheds were produced and shown on the table. The DEPUTY Mayon : We refused to accept the plans. Only two or three voted for thelamendment, and it was declared to be lost. The minutes were then carried in their entirety. CONFERENCES OF OFFICIALS TO BE HELD. He believed he was right in saying there were portions of work which the Paving and Sewerage Committee knew noth- ing whatever about. It was things of that kind which made people say "It's the Warrington Corporation. They have just finished this corner, and other men come and pull it up again." The Tramways Committee had so constructed the tramways in Wintnarleigh-street that it had been necessary for the Paving and Sewerage Committee to spend a very considerable Sdnl of money. He (Couneilior Tinnion) felt very muck annoyed , and grieved when he saw the flags being pulled up when the work might have been done last week. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Cairo-street Chapel. The Rev E W Lummis. M.A., preached the second of the series of sermons on '6 Unitarianism " in the above place of worship on Sunday evening. The subject on this occasion was " A Unitarian's thought of Gad," and it was dealt with in an able manner. Bold-street Wesley Guild. On Monday evening the monthly devotional meeting of the Wesley Guild, in connection with Bold-street Wesleyan Courch, was held in the church parlour, when a paper was read by Mr It Sutton, on " Man's relation to GA:" Mr Sattoo's remarks were based on the first two words of the Lord's Prayer—" Oar Father "—and the care wish which the paper bad evidently been pre- pared, as well as the spiris of reverence which character iz sd its delivery, contributed much to make the meeting profitable in the highest sense t 3 everyone present. Canaiderable discussion followed the reading of the paper, the essayist's words on the " Father "as using she rod of afil.ction to correct erring children, being particularly commented upon. Sankey's hymns appro- priately selected, were sung, as well as the " " by Miss Allen. Mr J J Rolland was in the chair. The meeting closed wNo a " chain of prayer " and the sing- ing of the Doxology about 9.30 o'clock. St. Barnabas' Church. Services on behalf of the Universities Mission to Cen- tral /Uric& were held in St Barnabas' Churchou Sunday. The preacher at all the services was the Rev C G Town- ley, of Ulverston, who delivered interesting discourses on the work of the mission. In the course of his ser- mon at the evening service the rev, gentleman pointed Oil that the mission was foundrd in response to the appeal of David Livingstone who called upon the C burch through her Universities to go over and heir, him to heal that open sore of tna world." Great things had been done by the Imission for which they might well rej3ice. On , the site of the slave market at Z anzibar, the last open slave market in the world, a Catne Irad had been built. A native ministry which was formed of boys who had been released from slavery was being trained,and there were already four native priests and eight native deacons. There was a theological college with a staff for training the iaatives,and there were mission stations with !churches, schools,and hospitals, which were ever being added to. More workers and funds to meet the growing requirements were urgeatly needed and he appealed to teem for liberal suppars.—Collections were taken in aid of the mission and realized a satisfactory amount.

The Bishop of Liverpool at Earlestown. The Lord Bishop of Liverpool (the Right Rev Dr. I Chavasse) on Sunday morning and afternoon preached special sermons at the Newton-in-Makerfield Parish ' Church, Wargrave. The church on both occasions was exceedingly well filled, a large number being unable to fin! seats. His lordship preached from the text "No it is high time to awake out of sleep, for now is oar salvation nearer than when we believed." He said that the people of Lancashire were wide awake in some matters. They were wide-awake in matters of trade. wide-awake as to the state of the markets,and as to the best way in which to make money. They were wide- awake in mattrra of pleaeure, and they knew well how to invest what money they had and to obtain as much enjoyment out of it as possible. They were with- awake to their own interests. But many of those keen, far-sighted, shrewd men were asleep as to the affairs of G)d. At the conclusion the Bishop, on behalf of the Rector ad Churchwardens appealed for liberal contributions towards the church renovation fund. So far, he said, they had celleteed LSD and they needed £7O more to make the church absolutely free' from debt. Be hoped they would raise £lOO in order to provide hassocks for kneeling. Their generosity and love for the church was reflected very much in the fact that only in a short space of time they raised so much money to make the caurch beautiful. It was only three months ago he was there at a confirmation service and he heard then it was their intention to renovate the church. H 3 could not help expressing his delighted surpriae at the thorough manner in which the work had been accomplished. He was sure the people of Wargrave had great love for their church and were desirous of making it both comfortable and beautiful. The choir rendered most effectively the anthem " The sun shall be no mere E Woodward), Mr E Leyland presiding at the organ. In the afternoon • bright and attractive childrea's service was bell in the church. The col- lections throughout the day amounted to a trifle over £l5. In the evening the Bishop preached at St John's Church, Esrlestown, in aid of the Liverpool Cathedral fund. There was an over-crowded congregation, tem- porary seats having to be provided in the aisles, while many people were compelled to stand. The Bishop, after delivering a very able discourse on Advent, re- ferred in conclusion 1p the proposed Cathedral at Liverpool. He said the' Vicar and Churchwardens had consented most generously that the offerings of the con- gregation should be given tto the Cathedral for the Liverpool diocese. Re would only remark to them that although the Cathedral would be built in Liverpool as the chief city in the diocese, i would not only belong to Liverpool but to the whole of the diocese. It would be their Cathedral as ranch as that of the residents of Liverpool. It was his heart's desire that every man, woman, and child in the diocese should feel that the Cathedral belonged to them and that they had a stone or stones in it. It was his heart's desire that every man and woman, young and old, who, in years to come, might walk round its stately walls or kneel beneath its soaring arches, or listen to the word of God faithfully preached from its pulpit, might be able to say to their children and their children's children "I helped through God's grace to build tnat magnificent pile."

CATARRH 01- THE NOSE AND THROAT IN RELATION TO DEAFNESS.

Among the causes of Detfuess,catarrhal inflammation of the nose and throat are of great importance. The reason for this is easily found in the fact that the , Eustachian tube gives a direct passage from the upper . part of the throat behind the nose to the cavity of the , middle ear. Through this passage catarrh extends to , the ear, and produces changes in the membranes that lead to Deafness. A more complete description of deafness produced by catarrh,as well as by other causes, will be found in the "Journal for the Deaf," which can lie obtained free of charge on apolication by post to the Secretary, Regent's Park-road, N.W. This Journal also contains an account .of t large number of cases cured by the Drouet treatment, which is rapiily taking the place of other ma thods formerly used by Aurists. An illustration of complications of catarrh of the nose and throat,with Deafness of long standing, is presented in the case of Miss Fanny Stenning, of Arundel, Sussex. In this case both the catarrhal ailment and the Deafness were of chronie character. The oresence of mucous discharges in the nose and throat were most annoying, and there were ulcerations of a distressing character. The Doafness was so snrere that the ticking of a watch was only faintly perceived even when held close to the ear. The case, as submitted by the patient's Report Form, wa ; diagnosed by the Consulting Phyeician as postnasal pharyngitis, eustachitqs, with otitis media. The treatment prescribed was conducted by correspond- ence, and resulted in a cure, reported as follows : Westfield, near Arundel, Nov. sth. I am very pleased to tell you the medicine has done me so much good I shall not require any more. I can I hear a watch tick half way across the room. My voice has become clear and the discharge at the back of the nose has disappeared. It does seem such a relief alter being bad so long.—Your grateful Patient, FANNY STENNING. In addition to the'' Journal for the Deaf," the Drouet Institute will send free of charge to patients unable to call personally, a special Report Form, which allows them to submit their ease clearly to the Consulting Physician. Any advice by correpondence is absolutely free, and the Journal and Report Form are sent on mere application to the Secretary, Regent's Park-road, London, NW-

WESLEYAN FOREIGN M;SSIONS.

Anniversary at Runcorn.

St; Paul's Church.

The annual sermons in connection with the above mission were preached on Sunday at the St Paui's Wesleyan Church by the Rev R Forster. There were good congregations at all services, the rev gentleman Preaching most appropriate discourses. On Monday the annual missionary meeting was held. There was but a moderate attendance. Mr B Brown, Widnes, should have occupied the chair, but owing to Meese was prevented his place being filled by his son-in-law Mr Rawson. He was supported by the Revs A Wens, superintendent minister, H Morrison, J B Foster, G M Cobban, Foster, Messrs. A R Norman, F J Norman, R Li Posnett, S Taylor, T Simister, T Bootes, R Green- P T Lawton. and S Coventry. After devotional exercises the Rev H B Morrison said that they raised la s' year in their own district £725 9s which included £237 lie from their own circuit which was an increase of £l2 on the previous year. (Appiause). The sum of ..x.:12 16s Id bad been collected by the Women's Auxiliary Society which had been sent to the Mission fund in London direct. The young people had raised £123 which was very creditable indeed. Mr Taylor, local secretary said that the total amount raised by St Paul's and Brunswick 9chool was £149 for the circuit which was a considerable increase. The reports of the collections of the boys and girls, the classes in Brunswick Scheel and also collections by ladies connected with the church were also read by Mr Taylor and were of a most gratifying character. The Chairman regretted that his father-in-law was not present for he took a deep interest in all missionary work. It gave him much pleasure to be there for the foreign missionary work was vital to the life of the church. Home missionary work was all vary wel but no one with a New Testament in his hand cou:d dis- believe in the command to preach to all the nations. He was glad they in Runcorn were so strong. He con- gratulated them on their recent School Board victory. He wished they in Widnes were strong enough to achieve a similar performance, and give a religious education on Bible lines without bigotry and intolerance. Touch- ing on South Africa and China the speaker said he was progress- ingTebteeacdriylystillrang " The world for Christ," acd he trusted that other cir- thaelsmdiosnsiot cults would emulate the example set by Runcorn, l pleased s e d there that andat nh the rCyown rnke nwt .a s Tie Rev R Foster gave a , number of interesting reminiscences, during which he said there was some- thing in Methodism which helped te make workers. He was very hopeful of the DaiSSiOCIS in Italy. The Remish Church was bound to go down. It was contrary to body, mind and conscience. Facts were against it. They (the Wesleyan Missions) were not attacking or des- troying, but were taking the gospel of the Lord Jesus Christ—not the "strategy of truth "—but the pure simple gospel to their hearts and consciences which brought relief from their sins. (Applause.) The Rev GM Cobban, late missionary in the madras district, gave an interesting account of his labours in India. 'file popular religion of tae Hiedoee was idolatry pure and simple. They worshipped the idol as God. Every Hindoo was religiouS. A man would be treated as an outcast if he did not worship. They gave a great deal of ungrudging time—at least 10 or 12 times more than the average Christian—to religious worship. They went in families, grandparents and children many a weary mile for the blessing they could not possibly get. They worshipped by lying flat on the bare ground. The only voice that was telling them to forsake their idols was that of the Christian Missionary. Elindooisna and Mohammedanism had no message for the people. The people knew that the Christan religion was the best be- cause it produced the happiest men and women. Had they more money they cold do much more good. The work was going on steadily in the district where Mr R Posmtt's brother was working. Evangelists were, however, needed and for £25 an evangelist with his wife and family could be kept for twelve months. He constantly had to refuse applications from villages for evangelists. God had given them success in many ways and by different means the nitives were:etercught to Christ, He apt,eated to all to renew their efforts in Missionary work. Mr A R Norman proposed and Mr R H Posnett seconded, a vote of thanks to the Chairman and the ministers who had conducted the services. The collections that evening amounted to £lB 13s 111 making a tro•al for the year of £l6B Os 7d being an increase of £l7 173 ld on last year.

A social evening was spent by the members of the above choir in the Workingmen's Mission Schools, Thewlis-street, on Monday, the object being to pre- sent the conductor,Ofr S Hassell, with a baton. Mr Greenleaves occupied the choir. Mr Millington, in making the presentation, spoke very highly;ol the services rendered by Mr Hassell, and expressed the pleasure the choir had in making that slight recog- nition.—Mr Hassell very suitably responded. Solos, duets, and choruses were rendered by Miss Taylor (soprano), Mrs Hannon (contralto), and the mem- bers of the choir. The accompanists were Miss R Tilling, Miss Gough and Mrs F Atkinson. Altogether a very enjoyable evening was spent. Refreshments were provided, to which ample justice was done. Votes of thanks were passed to the Misses Birch, Strange, and Hassall for the admirable manner in which the repast had been prepared. "Love, Courtship and Marriage." A lecture-recital was given in the Spiritualist Hall, Bewsey-street, on Monday evening by Mr Will Phillips, of Manchester, editor of the '' Two Worlds," on the above subject. The meeting was promoted for the benefit of the Lyceum. Mr Stout occupied the chair, and briefly introduced Mr Paillips. The subject was treated in a very inter- esting and instructive manner. The lecturer first gave some of the many definitions of love, and fol- lowed with remarks upcn the noblest and purest forms of love—child love,varental lovel&c. On the subject of courtship be gave some excellent advice original and selected, and in dealing with marriage pointed out the importance of making a careful selection of partners, and the necessity for a thor- ough understanding of each others' traits acid char- acters if happiness was to be the result. The lecture was at times very humorous and at others equally grave, and the various points were illustrated by a number of readings from various authors, and also by the following songs : " Doctor Dan," " The Darkie's love," and the " Skippers of St. Ives."

THE EXAMINER.

DECEMBER 6TE 1901.

A feeling of disappoint- The Policy ment has resulted in smile Of Roosevelt. quarters from the staid and sober nature of the Message which President ROOSEVELT has sent to the' Congress of the United States. True, the PRESIDENT had prepared people for this by pointedly declaring that he would not in any matter change the policy of his predecessor. But there were two topics upon which even the policy of President McKim,cv promised to be Sensational. He promised, or threatened, legisla- tion on Trusts and reciprocity. Both of. these subjects have been tackled by the new PRESIDENT and it is evident that some kind of action will be taken with regard to them during the session of Congress. If the references to them are couched in mild and measured tones, it is quite possible that that only shews the PRESIDENT'S caution in I avoiding unnecessary alarm on approaching what , are admittedly subjects of great difficulty. At the same time, there may be more in the declarations of Mr. ROOSEVELT than meets the eye. He alr • proaches the question of Trusts with a studied , regard to the sensibilities of the capitalists, for he - defends them from much that has been un- justly and unwisely said against them. The PaEsiDENriapproaches the subject as the head of a great industrial nation, and therefore of , a great capitalist people; and he asks whether it would not be a wise thing, when these great cor- porations are formed, to see that they are not over-capitalised, and that the investor has good • security for his money, and is protected fronv various kinds of fraud which result from the

"crime of cunning." Most assuredly.' But thie ' is not exactly the kind of reform for which the worker and the small manufacturer hoped and the great capitalist feared. It may be,:however, that, having disarmed the saspicion and opposition Of' the honest capitalist, he will proceed to more drastic operations. He goes on to point e'itt what is quite true,that what these Trusts require is not suppression, but control. They are a greet public necessity—like the tarift,which most Ameri cans swear by—an outgrowth, an effect, and also ' cause of great growing industrial development. They bring their benefits, but they have also their evils; and what they require is supervisioe• Beyond this President ROOSEVELT Will not go et present. Granted the necessity for control aria supervision, the next necessity is information, col' lected in a calm, dispassionate spirit. This ma appear slow, but perhaps it may be the surest wa to reform in the long run, if the PRESIDENT honest; and his record points that way. Ile will therefore establish a department like our 00' Board of Trade, under a Cabinet Minister WO . will be designated the Secretary for Commerce and Industries, and it will be the duty of tbier official to keep himself informed of the workingl c'' the great corporations into which capital is BO combining. He does not say what kind of legir lation will follow in the interest of the workrae°' who will be more than ever at the mercy of ale . capitalists' combination. Perhaps with the fact! '. I that are accumulated the necessity and nature o' . this will become self-evident. In the meantime,* Protectionist sop is thrown to the workmen. '45, the manufacturer is protected from the coraPer - tion of foreign imports, the workman is to be Pre tected against the importation of Chinese le1)011: and peopl9 of low moral tendency, Anarchists/an those of an unsavoury reputation, those who naV7 ; not an intelligent appreciation of American just] tutions, a capacity to earn "an American living, I , [ and a certain amount of mosey to start This is supposed to save the workman from Undll competition; but how it is to be done does not seem clear at first sight. Like the reciproci.t9 which is to be introduced to modify high tart wi.th charges, it appears both incongruous in principila and unworkable in practice. It reaY America, for all that. Rather, an attempt mar. be made to work it out in the absence. of sae work ont I thing better.

THE clericals are pre- The New Education Bill and Clerical Claims. paring to present 0 their claims to the Government, whom they seem to take it for granted have committed themselves to the State support of their schools, more or less complete. CARDINAL VAUGHAN has issued a statement of the Catholic claim signed by him on behalf of himself and the Rotran Catholic Bishops of the Province I, of Westminster,in which care is taken to lay down the principles which ought to guide them on the question of the control of elementary and secon- dary education of the County Councils. The first of these is a sweeping one, which quite obviously abolishes the necessity for voluntary subscriptions or religious institutions, for " they take for granted that the payment of public moneys, whether derived from the rates or the taxes, will be made equitably to the maintenance of all schools fulfilling the educational conditions, irres- pective of creed." The second is like unto it, for it " considsrs it essential " that representatives of "the great educational interests that have grown up with the Education Department " should be co-opted on to the Education Com- mittees, to see that the clerical schools get their full share of the public money. It will be observed that it is also taken for granted that while " interests that have grown ‘up with the Education Department are to be thus tenderly cared for, the representatives of the ratepayer, and the greatest educational interest of all, the public schools, are quickly elbowed aside. True, they are to descend to this heterogeneous com- mittee of all the creeds, who will regard them as " their own," but nevertheless a bastard progeny; and lest a more favourable feeling should by some chance grow up Catholic representatives are to be on their guard. Each school association must therefore have one representative on the com- mittee. The councils who supply the funds may have a representation on the board of management 'for sanitary, financial, and scholastic purposes " in a porportion " not exceeding one in three." So that the priests and the parsons will have the teaching matter to superintend, and will be in- vested with such important powers as the appointment and dismissal of teachers, while the Council representatives will have the pleasure of paying the bill out of the rates and taxes. A truly ideal system of "Christian schools "—from the clerical point of view. Similar claims though not in such excellent detail, are made of the Anglican Church in resolutions passed at a joint meeting of the Diocesan Boards of Education tor London Rochester, and St .Albans, held at Westminster. A curious feature is disclosed in the statement of the Anglicans' case. It is an admission that the Voluntary system has failed. "Immediate and substantial relief to the voluntary schools, both in town and country," we are told, "cannot be longer delayed without immediate loss of many schools g, and hampered efficiency in all." There the murder is out. Notwithstanding doles and special privi- leges, the Volantary system cannot possibly hold out any longer; and lest it should be absorbed in the triumphant School Board system the latter is to be strangled and handed over half-dead to the tender mercy of the priests and the parsons to finish the operation. Perhaps the Nonconformist conscience will at last awake to see what its half- hearted acquiescence in the Voluntary system of Church schools is going to cost the nation in civil and religions liberty. Then it will begin to make unmistakable when it

is exerted.

Just fifty years ago this week important events Were happening in earis. On December end, the c arefully-arranged coup (Tete, the crime of • Napoleon 111. which Victor Hugo has branded everlastingly, was carried out with almost ridi- culous ease, and during the night nearly all the ‘ independent members of the Chamber were . arrested and sent to the castle of Vincennes.Victor Lingo, though the object of special search, escaped ;• and spent the next few days waniering through the streets, alternately saddened at the indiffer- ence of the classes which brought about the e revolution of 1848, and shocked by the needless ;..and savage butchery of the Faubourg S. Antoine. 1, It was on December 3rd, 1851, that A.lphonse . Laudin, the Nantuan Deputy, taunted with his official salary, mounted a barricade in face of Louis Napoleon's troops, sayinc, : "I will show • you how to die for twenty-five i francs a day !" For once, however, heroism ,?,• Was not contagious. corruption had too well pre- i• pared the way of violence. The crime was bound i, to succeed, but inevitable stages led from its corn • raission to the terrible retribution of Sedan. There iS nothing more tragic in all history than the sequence of events from December end, 1851, to Ssptember end, 1870. There are some, it is true, Who still applaud and wouli fain imitate the policy of the coup d'etat. " Strange is it," as M. Blowitz writes finely to the Times, "that fifty years after that murderous day men assemble at a. banquet in Paris to celebrate it, and fanzy that t',11,_51, thus stifle the voice of history on a day '"Paped by it as accursed, the only possible a,"vantage of which is that it whitewashed the s 4- th Brumaire. History is inflexible, and cannot be muzzled or brihed. Its everlasting curse, Whatever the effrontery of the assassins ®f liberty, stigmatises them in the indignant eyes of pos- terity." Hugo has mad it impossible to recall the massacre of the Faubourg St. Antoine without remembering also Sedan.

Sir Hiram Maxim draws a clear distinction between a steerable balloon, such as that with which "M. Santos-Dumont has won the Deutsch I prize, and a flying-machine proper; and it is well to observe the difference. The distinction most ' people feel inclined to draw is such as exists between what is an accomplished fact and what still exists in the realm of fancy ; but this is not Sir Hiram's view at all. He thinks that M. Dumont has got as far as it is possible to go with the improvements on the balloon,but that a great Tuture still lies before the flying-michine. The future is not so distant, either, as one might sup- Pose, for according to the great inventor we have the material and the motive power all ready, as he had found in the course of some ex- periments. As these were described to be like skating without skates, or learning to ride a bi- cycle without the bicycle, the exact value of the experiments is difficult to gauge. What Sir Hiram aims at is a machine which, lin all Natures flyers, ie heavier than the air, and depends for its suspen- sory as well as its propelling power entirely on dynamic energy. Petroleum motors have been already developed sufficiently light to propel .. Machines which flew like birds, and as soon as we Ja ascertain how this power is to be advantage- ously employe I then we shall be able to fly. It is all delightfully simple. Now that we have the Material and the motor we only want skill and Pluck, plenty of cash, and a manceuvring ground. Weil, that is easy. But the flying has still to be demonstrated.

, - Have the other asylums and enable the authorities to deal with fresh cases. The Asylums Board sought power to send cases direct from the Work- houses or their own homes to Winwick thus doing away with the expense of first sending them to one of the other asylums,but the Commissioners could not see their way to alter the agreement, contend- ing that advantage would be taken to send patients other than chronics, and for whom there was no proper accommodation. It will therefore be seen that the Winwick Asylum has been specially fitted for chronic cases and will be used exclusively for such patients. It is a sad commentary upon the times that such a huge establishment is required in Lancashire for the reception of lunatics who when once they enter its portals can never hope to leave it until death releases them.

The sad news reached Warrington on Thursday last week of the death from wounds received in action of Lieutenant John W Broadbent,an officer in the Imperial yeomanry, who was well-known here as the second son of Mrs Broadbent of Latch- ford. The war is responsible for many sad hearts and homes but how dreadful the constant loss of life really is we never realise until the name of some friend or acquaintance appears with its accompanying bald unsympathetic accuracy in the official casualty lists. Then the horror of war 'is brought home to us and we wish, as many thousands of anxious and grieving people do all over the British Empire, that the prolonged strife in South Africa was at an end. It is a bitter struggle unworthy of the life and treasure spent in it. The Boer race is almost annihilated while England has lost a terribly high proportion of the gallant fellows who have gone to the war there impelled by loyalty and devotion to the Mother Country.We are sure that the syMpathy of all our readers will be extended to Mrs Broad- bent and her family in their sudden, sharp, bereave- ment, together with the earnest hope that Fate may be kinder to those of Warrington's sons who are still at the front.
