MATTERS OF MOMENT

A Provincial Letter.

Nineteen hundred-and-two will be prominent in history as the Coronation Year of King Edward the Seventh, and in all parts of the Provinces may already be observed preparations which brook no delay, for duly celebrating the great event. It has been understood for months past that the Corona- tion.will take place in June, probably on the 26th and in fixing this month for to ceremony the pre- cedent set by Queen Victoria has been followed. Her late Majesty ascended to the Throne in June and was crowned on the 28th of June in the follow- ing year. In as much as the last Coronation took place sixty-four years ago two generations may be said to have passed in the interval. So that there is no wonder the r...eremonial,especially as it affects the rights of individuals, has to be carefully re- considered and settled. The whole country will look forward to the Wronation as a means of stimulating interest in our unique system of Con- stitutional - Government, and it will be a source of great satisfaction to the King and all his loyal subjects if it can be said before the evant comes round,that peace and prosperity have been restorel to South Africa.

--- As may have been gathered from previous notices in this weekly letter, the relations between miners and Coalowners, particularly in the South Wales district, are far from satisfactory. Mr A A Thomas M.P., for Merthyr Tydfil, has just been advising both•parties to settle their differences amicably, and not to encourage American competition by a general stoppage in the South Wales coalfield. The policy of stop-days, or " play " days, has brought matters to a crisis, and the "Times" correspondent said on Tuesday that the proceedings pending against the men's leaders appeared to involve a battle royal between employers and the South Wales Miners' Federation in respect of damages occasioned by the allege wrongful stoppage of work. The men's leaders now demand a Concilia- tion Board, with the maximum standard of wages; and the difference between the sliding scale ar- rangement and a Conciliation Board is, that under the former the sole determining factor in regulat- ing wages is the price of coal, whereas under the' latter the price forms but one, and not the most important factor. On the whole, Mr Thomas's ad- vice appears commendable and worthy of being acted upon, and while the miners would certainly do well to await the result of the appeals in the Aberdare cases before ordering other sop-days, the coalowners on their part may be asked to remember that Conciliation Boards have worked satisfactorily in other industriee, and why may they not in this?

Provincial Pat Stock Shows are to Smithfield what the skirmishers are to an army. The majority of the provincial, or: outpost contests have been de- cided, while the principal "engagement "in the Christmas campaign on the historic site at Isling- ton, is yet awaited. It is really wonderful how bravely the farmers who gravitate to these big annual shows manage to dissemble their misery. To the superficial observer.at Bingley Hall,Birming- ham,they appeared to be in the best possible spirits, notwithstanding the many reasons offered by their friends in a portion of the press why they should not be so. It has to be remembered, however, that the men who are mostly interested in the marvel- lous specimens of bovine architecture paraded on these occasions do not represent the class upon which agricultural depression usually has the greatest °effect. Me live-stock breeder is not to be in all instances confounded with the farmer who depends upon the produce of the soil for his liveli- hood. Certainly, farming and stock-raising are often combined,but it would be quite possible te be a stock-raiser without being an agriculturist. The cheapening of food stuffs, too, has rendered many of the stock-raisers iess dependent than ever on the results of field operations, though, as a matter of course, the run of a succuleat pastur- age is ' indispensable to their eventual success at the shows. It thus comes to pass that while agriculture generally may be floundering in a Slough of Despond,the stook-raising industry, though not a royal road to fortune, does not suffer so acutely from any prevalent depression. ' It may be mentioned in this connection as a notable fact, that the home supply of meat, generally ; speaking, shows no signs of expansion,although the I acreage laid down in pasture is extending. Indeed, if the home supply were not augmented by that from abroad, the result would be that some kinds of meat would rise to practically famine prices. But so long as the means of transit remain as they are, and so long as the refrigerating processes are efficient the home consumer need be under no apprehension that the prices of certain varieties of rash meat will rise.

From the Birmingham Cattle Show it is reported this week that the popularity of the Poultry Sec- tion is increasing. It was a happy thought to encourage at the great Christmas stock exhibitions an improvement in the breeding and feeding of poultry for the table, which at once opened up a vista of great possibilities for one of the minor cul- tures. The display of poultry on these annual occasions has now become a settled feature. Towards poultry-rearing for profit much attention has, in recent years, been directed. There are still critics who contend that poultry-breeding does not pay; but the weight of evidence is against that conclusion. Year after year we continue to pay enormous sums to our colonists,and to the foreigner for poultry. It is not very agreeable to think of these large amounts constantly finding their way out of the country ; and those who are best quali- fied to give an opinion on the subject tell us very distinctly that it is not at all necessary. It may be as well to remember, moreover, that within the last half-dozen years certain of the railway companies have done something, if not so much as may have been expsOted, or so much as they and other com- panies are yet willing to do, towards removing the difficulties which beset the disposal of the produce of English poultry-yards, by the introduction of easier rates.

--- Food Preservatives were much discussed, it will be recollected about the time of the "poisoned beer" scare some months ago, but the fact may not be so readily recalled that in 1899 a Departmental Com- mittee was appointed to inquire into the whole question of food preservatives. The committee, however, have now issued their report in the form of a portly blue-book; and it is obvious that such a production must bristle with controversial passages. This will probably be the case, also,when the recom- mendations come to be analysed by those qualified to speak for the trades most

===== 0002601_19011206_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
affected. As is well known, the consumption of preserved foods has grown to enormous propor- tions during the past decade or so and the processes invented are multitudinous. At this particular season of the year artificially preserved foods are in demand in all classes of households, from the highest to the humblest; but it is pi obsbly correct to say that toe consumer of modest means is the most important and the steadiest customer. So that the imposition of any limitation on the supply must have appreciable economic consequences. But even so the general health of the community must be safeguarded as far as possible, and it is with the view to strengthen these safeguards that the committee have reported. The general tend- ency of the report is to condemn the employment of certain preservatives now very largely used. Formalin, treatment with solicylic acid, the sophis- tication of milk by colouring substances and other matters are referred to at length, and the logical sequence of the committee's report, were its recom- mendations embodied in an Act of Parliament, should be the creation of a new sub-department of the Local Government Board, charged with the en- forcement of the new conditions regulating the we of preservatives and colouring matter in the foods concerned. Much, however, is expected from the development of refrigerating processes, which as yet are but at the preliminary and experimental stages.

MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN. The above useful mission held another of its weekly concerts at the mission room before a large and appreciative audience. There was a splendid representative programme consisting of solos, duets,and instrumental selections. On the proposi- tion of Mr Shaw seconded by Mr S Cross a vote of thanks was passed to the artistes. The Chairman, Mr C Johnson, was also accorded a hearty vote or thanks on the proposition of Messrs. Leathwood and Shaw. _

--- --- TEA-MEETING AT THE MERSEY MISSION. On Monday an interesting meeting in connection with the above mission was held, over sixty mem- bers of the mission choir and friends sitting down to a substantial repast. At the conclusion several speeches were given, Mr Shaw and Mr J Stokes eulogising the services of the choir in high terms for their assistance in public worship. Mr W Shaw, lay reader, thanked the choir for inviting the Sun- day school teachers, Mr R Shaw, organist, respond- ing on behalf of the choir and Mr C S Leaks for the band. BRUNSWICK MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT CLASS. In zonnection with this class, tlr T Perry, before a moderate audience, delivered a capital essay on " A retrospect of the nineteenth century," on Wed- nesday evening. The essayist very ably showed how the world had moved ahead during the last hundred years and how the condition of society had much improved. Evolution, he contended,was apparent in every sphere of lire, and doubtless the next hundred years would see equally import- ant changes. Taxes dough heavy to-day were enormously heavy at the beginning of last century The newspaper was taxed to prevent its popularisa- tion and food was extravagantly dear while open criticism was checked by Acts of Parliament. A vote of thanks was passed to the essayist an the proposition of Messrs Dodd and Coventry. Mr Simister presided. A HUNGRY MAN AT RUNCORN.—ALLEGED THEFT OF A HAM. At the Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday be- fore Mr E Imison (in the chair) and Mr R H Pus- nett, a middle-aged man named Henry Shaw, a stranger, was charged with stealing a boiled ham valued at 145 from the shop of Thomas Saunders, 52, Church-street, Runcorn, on Tuesday night. Another man Richard Dutton Johnson, was charged with receiving the same knowing it to have been stolen.—Thomas SaunClers, said that on Tuesday night he went outside to put the shutters up and on returning to the shop found the ham, a whole I one just boiled, missing. He did not know the prisoner.—Sergeant 'Gregory stated that at 10.40 the same might he met Johnson coming from a lodging house in Friar-street. He had under his arm the ham produced wrapped in paper. When questioned by witness he affirmed that he had received it from a man in the lodging- house, who told him to sell it. Witness then arres- ted Shaw and brought both to the police station. When charged Shaw admitted the theft.—lnspector Egerton asked for a remand to make enquiries about Shaw.—Both prisoners were remanded for a week.

_ RUNCORN NOTES.

INDERFUL BOOTS, I boast.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT] Before School Board matters sink into the oblivion from which they will only emerge at the end of the next three years, there are one or two littlelincidents connected with the late campaign which are so interesting that it would be a pity were they never to figure in all the glory of print. A story which is creating no little amusement at ' the expense of a strong supporter of the " rate- payer's candidates," is just now going the rounds. Meeting the Rev. Alfred Wells a short time before the result of the election was declared, the person in question sarcastically asked the rev, gentleman had he got his " bunting "in his pocket ready to display at the right moment. Quick as a flash the minister turned upon his would-be tormentor, "No ! I have not " was the reply " but have you got any crape in yours? You'll need it before long." The prognostication proved correct and the would-be wiL retired vanquished like the gentlemen he supported.

---- " Father " (Chambers) asked a little boy after the result had been declared on Thursday last, " would you not rather be Littler (smaller) than Cunningham. "I-my-son (fmison) I would (wood:'—comment wouid be superfluous with such an enligh:ened body of ratepayers as we now

Avery spicy piece of information has just leaked out which, if correct, shows the folly of counting one's chickens before they are hatched. So certain, it is stated, were the supporters of the " ratepayers candidates" of winning the day that arrangements had been male to ring a peal of bells in honour of the:victory. Bunting, flags and streamers, too, so the story goes had been brought to Runcorn to decorate the committee rooms. The fog horn, had cleared its brazen throat, and was to join in the jubilee of sounds, while, last but not least, there was to be quite a triumphal procession to Weston Point, the residence of the " cleverest man uf the election," Unfortunately the dream was broken by Mr. Posnett beating Mr Cunningham and Mr Norman beating Canon Wood. The humili- ation was complete, and all the preparations were in vain. It was a bitter draught to ewallow, and on some constitutions it acted so violently that they relieved their feelings with writing hasty and "naughty " epistles to some of the leading Pro- gressives. Somehow or other the Voluntaryists take defeat quite unkindly. It has been asserted that the elec- tion has partel fast friends. It is to be hoped not. They should copy the example of the Vicar. In the Parish Magazine he declares his intention "to work with the opposite side if the Progressives will allow him." Why cannot all bury the hatchet for, at any rate, another three years, in the same gener- ous manner as the worthy Canon ?

-- Congratulations to our " Mayor " ! Mr Posnett is now the proud possessar of a sturdy son and heir. The interesting event took place a few days ago, and it is worthy ot note that a similar piece of goof tortune has not befallen any of the gentlemen who have been at 1 the head of the city fathers for over fifty years. This auspicious event will be signalized in the customary manner, and it is expected that in a few days Mr Posnett and his batter half will be preseated, on behalf of the town, with a handsome miniature silver cradle in commemoration of the occasion.

The Thelwall nuisance is still as lively as ever. It troubles the inhabitants rather more than the councillors but it puts both in a pugilistic attitude. Unquestionally Mr Linaker h right in asserting that unless the Council do something definite it I will find itself ere long in an awkward predica- ment. It would be far more businesslike if the Council were to ask for a Local Government Board inquiry into the cause of the nuisance and so arrive at something definite.

As will be seen from the report in another column of the Council's proceedings it has been determined to spend £1,400 on the Common and £2,150 on water works. Nortuan-road, too, is engaging the attention of the Council and ere long it may be expected to become, in the words of the poet, "A, thing of beauty, and a joy for ever.' The first portion of trees and shrubs, which Sir John Brunner so generously gave to the town, is to be planted in this road, and doubtless, when the trees are in full foliage, it will form a splendid and fitting avenue to the park. It should, of course, be distinctly understood, lest some ill-natured people think that the planting should be com- menced elsewhere. than Norman-road, that other important streets will come in for their fair share of these "leafy ornaments" in due course. Artistic embellishments are now the order of the day every- where and Runcorn, apparently, is not to be one whit behind other towns in this respect.

The district nurse, who plays such an important and beneficial part in the amelioration of the town's ills, has now taken up her residence in Lightburn-street. Those who are likely to require her services had better make a note of the address.

-- Once again the Cycle Club has shown what a magnificent impetus it can give to the C.H.F. when it chooses to do so. The balance sheet has just been banded to me by the secetary, Mr Booth, and it shows that the Cycle Parade and bsnd Contest have realized no less a sum than .£62 7a Id, which has been paid in to the credit of the fund, This substantial amount reflects the highest honour on all the workers connected with the parade and Runcorn, especially the working class, will be under e debt of gratitude to its wheelists which it can never adequately repay.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SALVATION AR'IIY, RUNCORN. On Sunday the 74th anniversary:of the Salvation Army was celebrated by the members of the Run- corn corps in the Barracks, High-street. The ser- vices were conducted by Mr Ezerton Davison, of Liverpool. Special birthday greetings were read from the platform,one being from Mrs Commissioner Coombs, who opened the" citadel" 24 years ago. At the evening meeting Mr Egerton Davison gave a stirring address. There were fair congregations, and three converts were received. The collections were very satisfactory. ACCIDENT AT RUNCORN. On Saturday evening, about 10.30, an accident occurred at the drapery premises of Mr Hill, Eger- ton-street, Runcorn. It appears that a London and North-Western Railway goods lorry was waiting to convey to the station some theatrical property from a house opposite Mr Hill's premises,and when the horse was 'being turned round it suddenly shied. The lurry was forced backwards on to the para- pet,and crashed into Mr Hill's plate-glass window. The window was completely destroyed,and several persons narrowly escaped being cut by the falling glass. The window was insured. CONCERT AT WESTON SCHOOLS. In connection with the-Weston Church Schools the scholars gave a two nights'concert before large and appreciative audiences. The first portion of the programme consisted of items of various kinds which were rendered in a manner reflecting the hizhest credit on the artistes. The after portion was taken up by a selection from "The Arabian Nights Entertainment." The parts were filled by selected scholars which had been admirably trained by Mr Percy Robinson. The scenery had been painted with good effect by Mr Bankes. The Rev. H Stablestexhorted, during the entertainment„ the parents to maintain their schools by seeing that their children put in regular attendance. INTERESTING PRESENTATION AT NELSON-STREET. On Sunday in connection with the above mission the annual distribution of prizes to the scholars for regular attendance and good condue.t was held before an exceptionally large gathering. There were no fewer than 120 children present and over SO of these were entitled to prizes. Airs Baker, Mough- land's-lane, had kindly consented to distri- bute the priz SS and her attendance was most heartily appreciated by everyone. After the prizes bad been bestowed upon the recipients, a vote of thanks was accorded to ...Mrs Baker, on the proposition of Mr Bayley, seconded by Mr Lam- bert. and supported by Mr J Hayes.—Mrs Baker, in responding, said it was a great pleasure to be with them. She fully appreciated the difficulties of the teachers and eulogised their efforts. Mission work, she thought, was one of the most important branches of Christian work. The service closed with the Benediction. It should be mentioned that Ithe prizes were given voluntarily by the officials connected with the, Mission. HALTON SONS OF TEMPERANCE—ROSE OF HILTON DIVISION (974). A well-attended meeting of the above association was held on Saturday night in the Wesleyan Trinity Church schoolroom. SEATS IN COUNTRY LANES FOR CONSUMPTIVES. Mr H Wood Liverpool Sanatorium, Kiagswood, via Frodsham, wrote to Mr Ashton that it would be a great kindness to the patients of the Sanatorium if permission were given to him to place seats about the country lanes. Many patients could only walk a short distanc and got tired quickly. He wished to be allowed to place four or five between Kingswood and Moulilswortho also on the Alvanley-road, the Ridgeway-road, the, Overtoc-road, etc. He would set them back from the road and keep them in good repair. Mr Diggle said there was no reason why seats should not be placed on the roads. Mr Horton said it was a suggestion which required coasideration. He did not think it was desiraole to have persons sitting on the road side for infection chiefly arose from the patients themselves. As an authority they should not encourage them to occupy roads any more than really necessary. It might prove an injustice to the people of the neighourhood and they should provide means themselves where they might take exercise. Mr Ellison said they sat at the present time on the roadside in parties of seven at a time. They could not belkept off toe highway and it would be bitter to pro- videltbem with seats. Mr Woodward said there was not much room on the road ;side to provide seats. There would have to be recesses made. Dr Adams said the main thing was whether the patients expectorated. If so it would be loaded with bacilli which would be found round the seats. Tais should be more or less self-contained. In view of the fact 'that the chief danger was by the sputum, there should be some restriction placed upon it. Alter further discusion Mr Woodward proposed and Mr Talbot seconded that a committee consisting of Messrs Linaker' Talbot and Williams be appointed to consult with the medical man in charge, and report to theiCouncil. I HE ALLEGED FRAUD ON A

WARRINGTON TRADESMAN.

Prisoner Committed for Trial. At the Warrington Police Court on Saturday, before Messrs T Birchen (presiding) and R T Fairclough, Relph Plant, jun, of Pit-lane, Wolstanton, Staffordshire, was charged on remand with having, with inteet to defraud William Breward, earthenware dealer, Buttermarket- street, Warrington, on various datss in April, 1900, induced him by false pretences to accept certain valu- able securities to wit bids of exchange for £24, £3O, and £3l 17s. Mr Arthur Browne, ef Warrington, pro- secuted, and prisoner was represented by Mr T B Sproston,Newcastle-under-Lyme. William Breward, earthenware dealer, Buttermarket- West said that on the 7th of April he received a wire from the prisoner which he subsequently destroyed. He wired back to the prisoner and on the same day prisoner came to see him. He produced invoices dated April sth and 6th. 1900 and told him that he wanted to dispose of the crates mentioned in the invoices. He said "they had plenty of stuff about ".but they had to pay a partner out on Saturday nacraing; and taey had to find £350.f0r this purpose. Witness asked himishere the goods were and he said they were packed up at Lougport sta- tion,and they would te placed to his witness') 0-der the following day. He also produced way-bills from the rail- way company but witness did not know what had be- come of them. He showed him two bill forms which he filled in witnesses presence. He then wrote out the bill of exchange dated April 6th. for £26 14r. Witness was going to pay. him by cheque but prisoner said it did not matter, the bill would do instead. Before that date witness had never accepted a bill. He signed his name accepting it. He did so in consequence of it being stated in the invoice that the goads were at Longport Station. Prisoner then left him and took away the bill. On Monday April 10th he received another wire in consequence of which he went to Bank Quay Station, in company with Mr William Farnival. At the station be met the prisoner and aMr Baxter. Prisoner said he had another lot of gripe's to dispose of, better than the last. Witaess thereupon asked prisoner ' to go to his house and they all proceeded there. Prisoner produced the two invoices dated April 9:h. Witness read them through and pointed out one particular crate which be did not want. Prisoner persuaded him to take it, saying he would make him a reduction or 31 1, per &zeta on the crate. He asked prisoner where the goods were, and he said they were at Lengport Station. He added that he had plenty of crates at Longpert Station. The trade discount was 10 per cent., and prisoner agreed to give him an extra 5 per cent, as an inducement to purchase. Prisoner pro- duced two bill terms and Mr Farnival pointed out that they were made out for goods received. Prisoner said they were always made out that way, and the goods would be placed to his order at Lengport Station the next moraine. Witness wrote accepting the bills for £3O and £3l 17s. Witness subsequently received from the Railway Company three crates of goods. One of the crates contained the goods specified in the invoice, but the others did not. In consequence witness wrote to the prisoner but received no reply. He went to the Potteries on the following Friday and saw the prisoner. He told him about the contents of the crates being wrong and he said there had been a mistake in th 3 marking of them, or something to that effect, but the packer had been to the station and mede it right. Prisoner wanted to sell him more goods on the same terms and witness said he would unpack the others before be boueht any more. Witness went to Longpert station and inquired for the crates but had not time to stop and leek if there were any there. There ought to have been nineteen crates at the station, according to the invoices. The three crates he had received were worth Coeur. £l9 or £2O. He afterwards received seven more cratea the contents of which were worth 10; per crate. None . of the crates coutairied the goods specified on the in- voices. He sent them back to the station, but some weeks after wards acces ter] them. Witness subsequently received the circular produced calling a meeting of creditors of the prisoner. In the meantime he had written several letters to the prisoner and he went to see him. Prisoner said witness would be all right, he would see him paid before anyone. Witness subse- quently received a notice from the Official Receiver stating that prisoner had filed a petition in bankruptcy. He bad never received a penny of dividend. He had to pay the bills for the good!. Cross-examined : He did net know that April 7th wag a Sunday. Witness was not quite sure as CO the exact date of prisoner's visit, The invoice of April 6th wall not receipted. The bill for £26 14s was the exact value of the invoices of April sth, which was for five crates. He was prepared to pay cash for the crates but Plant said "If you're not flash in money you can give me a bill." He was not prepared to pay cash for the goods mentioned in the invoice of April 9:h. It was admitted that Breward had an interview with the prisoner after the StlMnions had been issued with a. view to settling the case. William Farnival, general dealer, 53, Bewsey-street, spoke to an interview between Breward and the prisoner. Plant said he had a quantity of goods to dispose of as he had a partner to pay out. Be said they were a good dea below the ordinary discount. Witness asked Plant why! ordinary cheques:would not do. Prisoner replied that the bills of exchange were as good as money. He said the goods were ready packed at Lengport station. Albert Elwin Lake. goods agent for the London and North-Western Railway Company, Lengport Staff erd- shire,said there were no crates of g3nria belonging to the prisoner at the station on April 6th. 1900. Cross-examined On April sth there were five crates consigned to Loneport station to the order of Breward. Oa the 9:11 April there were five crates consigned by the prisoner to prosecutor. Re-examined : Between sth April and 10th April there were never nineteen crates. Harold Moore, binkers' clerk for Birmingham and District Bank, said the three bills produced were paid into the prisoner's account. Sergeant Williams of the Staff erdshire psliee stationed at iWolstanton said he left a summons for prisoner at his residence with his sister on June 19:h. In the even- ing he saw the prisoner in the street, Be said "I quite expected it and it would not have surprised me if it had been a warrant. I admit it, but it was only an ordinary business transaction. I cannot help it the business bag failed lam not the only one' that's in it. " Witness re- ceived the warrant produced on July 2ad. He could not execute the warrant as prisoner had left Wolstan- ton. By Mr Sproston . He had heard that there were other partners in the business besides the prisoner. Sergeant Williamson said prisoner surrendered to him at Warrington Police Station on the 23rd of November. Witness read the warrant to him and he replied " I have nothing to say." Prisoner was committed to take his trial at the Liver- pool Quarter Sessions. Mr Sproston applied for bail and said the prisoner was in business with several partners and the concern failed. Certain negotiations took place with a view to an amicable settlement of the present case and it was not in consequence of the present proceeding that the prisoner left the country. Wnea he came back to this country he learned that a warrant had been issued and acting on his (Mr Sprostores) advice be surrendered. Prisoner was allowed bail in one surety of £lOO and himself in £lOO.

FIRE ESCAPES.

_ J E Enollys, assistant secretary to the Local Govern. meat Board wrote under date 2ad December, as follows :—" I am directed. by the Local Government Board to advert to your letter referring to the proposed provision of fire escape staircase at the Runcorn Union Workhouse, and to state that in the absence of a plan showing more generally the effect of providing these staircases, and their position relatively to the wards the Board must reserve consideration of the matter until the return of their Inspector, who at present is on sick leave." The Clerk said it was a question whether they should get further pleas or wait until the inspector got better. the Chairman : Better wait until he gets better. Mr Woodward said that as no limit was said as to time in considering the estimates, it might prove awk- ward should toe markets rise or fall as to whether the contracts would hold good. Admiral Jones Parry pointed out that the contracts were subject to the approval of the Local Government Board and they would have to wait their time.

VACCINATION CIIARGES. The Clerk to the Headington Union, Oxford,for warded a resolution passed by that Board requesting the L ocal Government Board to amend their Vaccination Order of 1898 with a view to reducing the present schedule of fees allowed to public vaccinators and vaccination officers, such fees being coosidered exorbitant, and asking for the support of the Board. EARLESTOWN BAPTIST CHURCH•

Recognition Meeting.

recet

said

Special recognition services were held in the Baptist Church, Earlestown on Sunday. There were fairly good congregations, the preacher being the Rev D S Tnomas, the newly appointed Rector. On Monday evening the Chapel Anniversary was celebrated by a tea meeting, and public gathering at which the new Pastor was publicly welcomed to the ministry of the Church. The tea was well attended, as also was the after meeting. At the latter Mr F G Aveline of Preston, presided and he was supported by the Rev J S lieghes of Hill Cliffs, Williams of Corwen. Green (Secretary of the Liverpool Baptist Union), CE Dave, D S Thomas and Mr G Dring. The Chairman said he was gratified at the honour accorded him in asking him to preside at that meeting, the annual meeting of the Earlestown Baptist Church. He was described as at one time an active member of that church, but if scrubbing the floors, painting, and stoking the fires consti- tuted an active member, then he could lay claim to that description. He was the secretary in those days, and the secretary was always the man who lid the most work and got the least pay. In those days they performed the duties in their turn, and somehow his turn came round very often. He did not look butt upon those incidents with any degree of regret or shame,for they were very happy times. He referred to the Co-operative Hall and the Green room in Market-street. The church was formed into a church in 1875, and he distinct y re- collected when the Rev H S Brown and several of his Deacons came up and they met together in Market-street and there they formed the first Baptist Church in Earlestown, and the number of members was 16. He took it they were met to- gether that night on the anniversary of the Baptist Church, and also for the publie recognition of the Baptist minister. He supposed they werelnot at all ashamed of that. There was nothing to be ashamed of in their denomination. Tney had something like 400,000 members in the British isles, and something like 730,000 scholars in their schools. These num- bers were comparatively small compared with some of the other denominations, but still they were not to be despised. He did n3l know that they need describe themselves as feeble folk. However feeble they might be they must remember that their principles were founded upon God's treth,and that they had their dwellings like the conies, amongst the rocks In America their denomina- tion was very nearly the largest, and judging from the very rapid progress they were making there, the time would come, he was satisfied, when they should head the list. The had 51 millions of mem- bers, and if they took the world at large they must put on at least another 600,000, so that altogether they would have between 7 and S million members. It they wanted to get on at all they must strictly and closely adhere to their Baptist principles and if they did -not they would for all practical pur- poses g 3 spark out. For their own sakes he asked them to be true at all costs to that which bad been committed to their charge in Earlestown. Baptists had always been on the side of liberty of consci- ence. That was important not only to them as Baptists, but to all their fellow men, whatever their views might be.:They knew the attempt that was being made on the part of sacerdotalism to capture the schools. As a Baptist community they should be in the fore front of this fight for freedom. He hoped and trusted from this time forth begin- ning afresh with a new pastor, they would be more faithful in their trust in the future than they had been in the past. (Applause.) The Rev J S Hughes of Hill Cliffa, said his first acquaintance with that church dated back some- thing like 10 years, when there was a recognition meeting on the occasion of Mr Cettam's introduc- tion as pastor. He had not been in the church very long before there was a very lively scene.and he was thinking that if the same parties had been present that night, when the Chairman was making his address, there would have been a repetition of that scene- But still he thought they had a right, in courtesy, to state their opinions and principles. He congratulated them upon the beautiful condi- tion of the church, and thought there was a bright future before them with a new minister. He thought it was much wiser for a minister to be left alone to find out the failings of his flock, and the onger he was doing it the better. Ntr Thomas had been too long in the ministry to be disturbed by the sharpest criticism or gossiping tongue. That was a recognition meeting, in which they were metl to recognise the union which had taken place be- tween them, and that they publicly recogniee Mr Thomas aa their minister, and that henceforth they would have certain claims upon him and he would have certain claims upon them. He then proceeded with a long exhortation on the duties of a congre- gation towards their minister. 'The Rev H C Williams. of Corwen, also spoke in eulogistic terms of Mr Thomss, with wham he had had an acquaintance of 26 years. The Rev C R Green believed they had made a choice directed by the great bead of the church. Mr Thomas was a man of more than average ability. They must support him in every possible way, and they would find as the happy months and 'years rolled by that God was in this matter. (Rear, hear.) The Rev D S Thomas said he felt that a speech from him would be out of place, as so many friends had come to take part in tse meeting, and so many kind things had been said that it struck him he ought to say something in recognition, and yet he did not f eel inclined to do that. Perhaps he ought to say that he was there as a Baptist minister, and that their invitation to him meant that he should be the advocate and maintainer of Baptist princi-

pies. The Rev C E Dove extended a welcome to Mr Thomas as a co-worker in the great influence of religion. If he could help Mr Thomas in any way ' he should be glad to co-operate with him in spread- ing Scriptural holiness in Earlestown. He hoped they would stand by their minister and that they would attend church not only in the evening, hat in the mcrning. (Applause.) The meeting terminated with the usual votes of thanks.

INTERESTING EXHIBITS AT WAR- RINCITON MUSEUM.

Relics and Curios from South _Africa.

There is at present on view at the Warrington Museum an intereiting collection of relics and curios from the war in South Africa lent by Captain F. M. Appleton, Private A. Ashton (late Imperial Yeomanry), and other members of the Volunteer Service Company of the let V. B.P.W.V. South Lancashire Regiment. The exhibits have been carefullY arranged in six cases under the supervision of Captain F. M. Appleton. They have been placed in the Art Gallery, anu will remain there for about a month longer. The first Case calling for attention contains the silver mounted loving cup presented by the officers, non commissioned officers and men of the Volunteer Service Company to the Mayor and Corporation of Warrington It is made from the case of a naval twelve-pounder shell, fired at Gracskop, where the Company formed part of a detachment of the regiment holdiue the Ladysmith-Pretoria railway under Major F. A. Adam, second in command let South Lance- shires. There is also in the same case a cigar lighter presented to the officers' ireas, Warring. ton Vol inteers by three officers of the South African Company (Captains Appleton, Crosfield and Cary). The sword presented to Captain Appleton is also exhibited and there are also two illuminated addresses, one pre- sented to each member of the Volunteer Active Service Company by the townspeople in recognition of their valour and patriotism in volunteering for and serving their country in South Africa, and the other presented by the Mayor of Caps Town to all the Volunteers from the Empire. Accompanying these exhibits are four photographs showing the uniforms worn by the local Volunteers in 1798, 1359,1E63 and 1896. Chief interest will be centred in the relics and curios. These comprise a miscellaneous collection of article 4, including Kaffir bead work, a Kaffir head-dress and banjo, ostrich eggs. a soldiers lantern, field glasses, shells, rifles, bullets, Boer expanding ballets, Boer prisoners letters and telegrams, a document containing the autograph signature of ex-President Kruger, and documents stipulating the rations for Boer prisoners. There are numerous other relics which cannot fail to be of interest. _ .

In close proximity are cases containing a collection of photographs of officers and msn connect d with the South Lancashire Regiment, more particularly those associated with the Volunteer Ssrvice Company. In addition there are photographs illustrating camp life and places visited by the Volunteers. Sadder pictures are rhose showing the graves of local Volunteers and officers and men of the South Lancashire Regiment. . .

The exhibits have already attracted numerous visitors to the Art Gallery arid early opportunity should he taken rf inspecting these interesting souveoirs which the members of the Volunteer Active Service Company have brought home with them from ti4e seat of war.

HOLY TRINITY CHURCH

WARRINGTON.

Sale of Work.

A sale of work in aid of the General Purposes Fuld was he'd in the Holy Trinity schools, Museum-street,on Thursdaylast week. The onesiog ceremony was perform- ed by Mrs Lyon,Applet-m pr(smce of a large attendanze. The room had been effectively decorated and presented a bright and attractire appearance. Lady members of the congregation assisted at the stalls:,

... in opening the proceedings the Vicar (Rev EC E Carleton) explained the object of the sale. He said that no doubt many of them would know that they had a useful mission in a retired part of the district known as Fact r 7 Yard. They had been allowed to Use the premises free of expense, for many years by the Misses Wilson-Patten. In this mission Sunday schools,mothers' meetings and girls' clasaes were held, and latterly there had been services on Sunday evenings conducted by a number of laymen. The only funds at their disposal tor carrying on the work had been the proceeds of the annual rummage sale and owing to , the way in which the sale had been sup- ported, and the exce'l?nt management of the finances by their friend Mr .Halton, who acted as treasurer, they bad hitherto been ab'e to carry on the work successfully without incurring any very serious liability. This year there had been rummage sales in several other parishes, and consequently there was a scarcity of the article known as rummage. It was there- fore decided to have a rummage sale supplemented by a sale of work. They were sorry to break a record which they were rather pleased with themselves about —that they had not had a sale of workror anything like a bazaar for nearly twenty years. If this sale ras well supported they cculd rely upon it that the funds would not be squandered. Any surplus would be put aside to meet contingencies that might occur in rears to come' It was also hoped that the proceeds would be sufficier.t to enable the treasurer t 3 make a gran: to St. Lu're's Curacy Fund. Mrs LYON in a brief speech. declared the sale open.

_ On the motion of Councilor FORSHAW, seconded by Mr riALTON. a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs Lyon for opening the sale. At interals during the afternrol mu4eal items were given and in the evening a short concert Wal held. The proceeds of the sale amounted to nearly £lOO.

WINWICK ASYLUM. To be opened at the End of the Year. Tne annaal meeting of the Lancashire Asylums Board was held last week in the C)untv Hall, Pieiton. Alderman W S Barrett (Manchester) was unanimously re-elected chairrnan,and Hr T H Jenkini (Salford) vice- chairman._

The Chairman informed the members that Winwick Asylum would be opened for patients at tbe end of the year. The Chairman moved the adoption of the report of the deputation appointed to wait on the Commissioners in Lunacy, with reference to the Board's agreement that the Wicwick Asylum should be wholly a chronic asylum, and filled exclusively, by transfer from the other asylums, with chronic cases. The report stated that the deputation urged that chronic cases should be allowed to be sent to Winwick Asylum direct from workhouses or their own homes, thus doing away with the expense of first sending them to one of the other asylums. The Commissioners, however, contended that if chronic patients were received into Winwick Asylum direct, advantage would be taken to send patients other than chronics, and for whom there was no proper accommodation, thus placing the medical superinten- dent in the invidious position of having to decline to admit them. This being so, they could not see thier way to alter the original agreement. The deputation also mentioned the undertaking to remove the tem— porary buildings, containing 200 beds, at Rainhill on the , completion of Winwick, and received the Commissioners' sanction to retain them for a further period of five years, _

1- Dr -Lev, the medical superintendent at Prestwich, having resigned owing to failing health, it wag decided to grant him a superannuation allowance of £l,OBO, be- ing two-thirds of big salary and emoluments. Mr B Carver moved that Dr Murray, senior medical assistant at Prestwich Asylum. be appointed to Dr Ley's post at a salary of £6OO per annum ; bat Mr Fit r- herbert Brockholes moved an amendment that Dr Perci- val, medical superintendent at Whittinetarn. be appointed, Both gentlemen had sent in applications.— After considerable discugsion, the amendment wan carried by 27 votes to 10.—An amendment, proposed by Mr W W B Hulton, that the appointment shou!d be thrown open at least to all the senior assistants at the asylums, was also defeated.—A substantive motion that Dr Percival be transferred from Whittingham to Rain- hill at his present salary (£1,000) was then carried.

NORTHERN UNION COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP.

YORKSHIRE V. CHESHIRE. Tg YORK9EIRENTEN OUTCLASSED.

The second of the Northern Unioa County Championship matches of the entrant season was played at Stockport on Saturday, Cheshire being the opponsnts of the broad- tared county. The result was anything but flattering to the York- shiremen's self-esteem, for instead of winning the match by their forward play, which most of their friends hal thought they would do. they were emphatically beaten in that de- partment, and Outclassed in the back division at all points except full back, and they lost the match by 13 points to 3. The Yorkshire team were accompanied to Stockport by the good muster of county officials and friends, and before the match they were entertained to luncheon by the Cheshire Comrnittes at the Brookfield Hotel. The match itself was favoured by fine but dull weather, the only draw- back being that darkness set in so early that it was ex- tremely difficult to see the last quarter of an hour's play. There would be between 4,000 and 5,0 JO persons present when play started, and the teems turned out promptly at three o'clock as originally selected with the exception that D Foster. of Castietord, took the place of Starks, al previously announced. The Yorkshire Committee elected Kemp captain of the team, on the ground that he had not bad that honour before. though in point of seniority Rigg, who was left in charge of the Yorkshire back division, should have led the team. Houghton ceptainel Cheshire, and beating Kemp In the toss decided to allow Yorkshire to play down the sligth slope, and with the bveze in their favour to start with. Hutt took the ki3k off, and for a few minutes there was nothing in the p'ay to call for notice. Auty had an opening for a sharp run which he spoiled, however, at the finish by passing to an opponent (Trotter). Marsden was kicked in stopping a wheeled scrummage and the game was delayed for two or three minutes, but he was, fortunately, able to resume. Murgatroyd next had a decent run for Yorkshire but. as In Auty's case, the pars went to are opponent. Ther- was a lot of marking. kicking, and rushing of an inconse quential character. Taylor, too, had a shot at goal for off side. Cheshire having to touch down. The first bit of really good football in the match among the backs came from Jolley. Richardson. and Warder, but Taylor was at hand when re )uired, and good kicking by Auty put Yorkshire in a position to score the first substantial point of the match. A scrummage todk place in the Cheshire ‘• 25." and 3lar (idea , passing out to Grace, the Leeds half dashed away at full speed and scored a well-deserved try. the defence being com- pletely beaten. Kemp took the shot at goal, but failed to send the ball over the bar. Alter this reverse Cheshire put an extraordinary amount of energy and dash into their forward play.and they immedi,. ate's, pressed Yorkshire beck to their lines by this means' They were kept out for a while, but Richardson finally go away and made a beautiful run, in the course of which he tricked one defender, and was being pulled down from be- hind by another when he passed to Butterworth, who went over the line with a Yorkshirernan on his back and scored, This was a magnificent try, and too much credit cannot be given to the performers. The point was got near touch, but Farmer capped the performance by scoring a brilliant goal. Cheshire pressed hard again until two or three minutes before the interval, when Yorkshire bad a chance, which Grace spoiled by holding the ball too long. At half-time the score was —Cheshire, 1 goal and 1 try (5 paints) : York- shire, I try (3 points). As Cheshire now had both the slope and breeze In their favour, there were small hopes that Yorkshire would pull off the match, for the play of the Cheshire forwards, after York- shire had scored their first try, had come quite as a revel- ation to the visitors. Forcer restarted, and the first item 'worth mentioning was a shot at goal by the same player, which went under the bar.(for Yorkshire to touch down. Then Laver& who had keen weak throughout, had a kick charged by Jolley, who rushed on and prevented Taylor covering the centre man's mistake. it was obvious, from the way Cheshire were playing, that a score would not be long delayed, and there was no surprise when a combined attack by Jolley, Richardson, Hodgson, and Butterworth resulted in the last-named easily beating both Rigg and Taylor, and scoring a splendid try, for Farmer to kick another goal. Next Yorkshire were beaten easily in a loose forward rush, and Cram picking up passed to Robinson, who got over with the home team's third try, Farmer this time failing with the goal shot. There were many attacks by Cheshire after this, but they were frustrated, chiefly by the magnificent defence of Taylor, and Auty's serviceable tackling should also be mentioned in this connection. A few minutes from the finish Yorkshire spurted. and the backs had a chance of a score, but it was lost by an absurdly weak attempt at pass- ing by the centre three-quarters. The eld came in darkness with the vote ,—Cheshire 2 goals, 3 tries (13 points) ; York- shire 1 try (3 points).

A free sample will be sent on application to THE MAZAWATTEE TEA CO., LIMITED, Tower Hill, London, E.C.

THE REV. JOHN GOULD AT WARRINGTON.

Lecture on "Charles Kingsley."

The Rev. John Gould. who conducted the recent Free Church Mission ia Warrington with marked success, re•visited Werrinzton on Sunday, and conducted the anniversary services in connection with Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. There were large congregations, the evening service being very well attended. Collections were taken on behalf of tne Trust Fund. _ _

-The •john Gould &livered an intensely inter- esting lecture on " Charles Xmzsley " in Bewsey-roid Wesleyan Church on Monday evening. There was only a small congregation, Mr R G Elenstiall presided, and there were alsa on the platform in addition to the lecturer, the Rev Theodore Bishop, Rev C H Hocken, Mr Bailey, and Mr E Place. Mr Wadsworth presided at the organ.

After devotionaf exercileS, the CHAIRMAN briefly introduced the R2V. John Gould.

- „ The LECTURER, at the outset, sketched the early life of Charles Kingsley and referred to s:me of the early impressions which affected Kingsley's future career. Mention was made of his religious doubts during his college career, and of the influenc,s which ultiinatEly led him to join the Church of England. He was subse- quently appointed curate of Eversley, in Hampshire, and afterwards rector. The Lecturer briefly, sketched the social condition of England at the time, and pointed out that when the Church was being most violently abused for the lethargy of her clergy, and her indiffere ence to the welfare of the people, Kingsley, who was greatly influenced by Frederick Denison Maurice, another Church of England clergyman, was one of the few far i seeing Church clergymen who were devoted to the wel- fare of the people. All honour. the Lecturer said, to this young cleryman.—Charles K;ngsley—who barely 30 years of age risked his projects and sscrified his health to put down the blind prejudices of the upper classes and compel them to take notice of the deplorable condi- tions of the thousands of starving women and oppressed workers weltering in degradation, the nature and cause of which few aired to inquire into. In 1849 Kingsley published the novel, " Alton Locke" in which he power- fully expressed his opinions of the social and economic questions of the time. Kingsley poured forth the whole force of his great passionate heart in wrath and indig- nation against starvation wages, reeking alleys, stifling workshops, and insanitary property. Had he done otherwise Kingsley might have been a bishop but he would not have been Charles Kingsley. At. St Paul's. he preached freedom, equality and brotherhood which greatly offended the clergy who re- garded him as the apostle of socialism. The lecturer passed in review Kingsley% literary productions describing the chief characteristics of " Yeast," " Alton Locke," " Flypatia " and " West- ward Ho." When Kingsley became chaplain to the Queen he gave up writing books of fiction and devoted himself to sanitary reform. Reference was made to Kingsley's appointment as professor of modern history at Cambridge in 1859, to his acceptance of Mr Glad. stone's offer of the canonry of Criester in 1869, and to the closing years of his life at Westminster. lii a closing eulogy on Kingsley's life, the Lecturer said Charles Kingsley was mite of the noblest and truest spirits the world had ever known. The CHAIRMAN said the Lecturer had given them a great intellectual treat and he trusted the lecture would make them better men and women.

Mr E PLACE proposed a vote of thanks to the Lecturer and Chairman.

I The Rev C HOCXEN. in seconding the vote, pointed out that the collection was on behalf of the trust fund. IThey bad about £4OO in hand, being the balance from the bazaar, but the necessities of the church required £5OO. They were now endeavouring to increase their funds so that they could safely proceed with many much-needed improvements. He expected that the decoration of the church would be commenced in the early spring. The vote was unanimously carried and the Chairman having acknowledged the compliment, the meeting ter- minated with the pronouncing of the Benediction.

THE WAR.

An Interesting Exhibit.

There is at present on view in the shop window of Messrs Boyden Bros., tailors, Sankey-street, a flag which was formerly used by the Ermelo Commando of Transvaal Boers, but was buried in the Usoto Spruit, where it was found by a Kaffir boy on the 17th June. The flag, which is bordered with red, brie and green, has a centre of white on which there is an oval shape depict- ing a lion in a sitting position, a young Boer, and under- neath a transport waggon and an anchor. The ti IF, is ornamented with gold tassels. It was secured by Ser- geant Fred Monks, of the Rifle Brigade, who sent it home to his brother at Warrington for safe keeping.

===== 0002601_19011206_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Local Militia Battalion Embodied.

Scene at Bank Quay Station.

On Monday afternoon the men of the 3rd (late sth) Battalion of the K ng's Liverpool Regiment left War- rington for Litchfield to uncltr2o training, prior, it is expected, to being diEprtcheci to South Africa. The battalion, numbering nearly 600 men and 22 officers, assembled at Orfora Barracks and proceeded to Bank Quay Railway Station, where two special trains had been chartered to convey them to Litchfield, prepara- tory to their departure for the front. The men, who where under the command of Colonel Bat:en, were played to the station by tbe band of the regment. The men quickly entrained, and as the trains were leaving the station, the band struck up the familiar tune of " Auld Lang Syne." the crowds outside the atation,who had accompanied the soldiers in their march through the streets, cheering hcartily. The men volunte,red for active service about 18 months ago, and the orders to embody, a week or two back, came as a distinct sur- prise to all. The soldiers looked fit and w 1 and seemed to be in the best of spirits.

The Late Lieutenant Broadbent.

Pulpit Reference by the Rev. F. A. Screeton

At Christ Church, Latchford, on Sunday morning, the Vicar (Rev I, A Screetem) made a briet rference to the death of Lieutenatt J W Breadbent. The subject of the sermon was " Judgment, Human, and Divine," the text being " The righteous judgment of God," Romans 2, V. In the course of his sermon the preacher said that the fallibility of human judgment bad seldom been more clearly exemplified than in the great war in which we were still engaged. Very few people in this country in 1899 had a true insight into the nature of the struggle we were entering upon. It was confidently expected that once beaters the Boer would give in. He had not been beaten and that was why he was so ready to go to war. Twenty thcusand British lives had paid the pen- alty of war—thousands of Boers lay huddled in lone graves and dear lited homesteads because the awful arm of war had drawn all veils aside and now the hidden realities stood out bare and naked for judg- ment. We talked about the horrors of war, the disaster of war—they were almost hackneyed phrases, and far Irene ecenes of action and shrieks of wounded and groans of dying they did not reaFze all that It meant, till, perhaps, one whom they had known, living in ens of their homesteads, was included amongst those who had bravely sacrificed life itself for the good of the country and honour of our flag. Then it came home to them more, and they thought cf the thousands of other homes wrapped in sorrow and desolation because they had lost their brave sons for the sake of the Empire and of those 'whom they had left behind in quietness and security. In their deep regret for the loss of one whose home was amongst their's and in their unrestrained sympathy for those who mourned him, they could not but feel proud of him and thcuaands of others who regarded not their own safety when th 3 country needed them and died for the land that gave them birth. Robert Hall, in his great oration against Napoleon Bonaparte when it was thought he was going to invade this country said !—" He has al- ready lived too long who survives the ruin of his country. Let us make one effort and if fall we must let us fall like men." Every war was a judgment. God was judging Briton and Boer. They appealed to the God of battles and that appeal had not yet been finally answered.

Memorial Service at Christ Church.

A very impressive memorial sercice was held at Christ Church on Monday afternoon. Evidence of the esteem in which the deceased officer was held was to be seen in the large congregation which assembled. Those present included the Adjutant and a number of officers of the Volunteer Battalion, officers from the barracks, and local clergymcn. The vicar conducted the services which consisted of a portion of the office for the Burial of the Dead with hsmns and special prayers. - CANON WOOD AND THE RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD ELECTION.

Writing in the " Runcorn Parish Magazine" this month the Vicar in his letter to his parishioners, referring among other subjects to the nchool Board Election says :—" The subject on which you will expect a few words from me, is, 1 am sure, the School Board Election. For the first time for many years the Progressives will have a majority.We mus accept chtertullythe ratepayers'verdict, and try to work with the opposite side if they will allow us Personally I have been attacked with a virulence to me extremely surprising. It is of course true,as I hastened to say, directly I knew it, that the 4d by which the School Board rate rose from 1893 to tee latter half of 1898, was not all due to the transfer of thb Wesleyan schools We thought it was and said so. But I could not have conceived that anyone would suppose 1 had purposely said what I knew not to be correct. And besides our argument does not de- pend upon the amount of the increase. So long as the expense of taking over a Voluntary School is substantial, the ratepayers must always regard the managers of Voluntary Schools as their friends. And as I certainly am not given to saying what I know to be false, so neither had I any sort of in- tention of attacking the Wesleyans. I should have as freely used the closing of a church school as an object lesson as I used the closing of the- Wesleyan school. From to the election just past, the matter lay dormant, and it was without a misgiving that we took up the some 4d 89 the amount. Owing to the questions never- having been raised at the Board, I had never'looked into the figures, Of course I ought to. have done so, and by not doing E 0 1 laid myself fairly open to a charge of carelessness. What a ' great pity, that we just lost Mr Cunningham ! so good and useful a member of the Board. I fear the town will suffer very much and I am sure I shall."

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The Water Controversy. The Proposed Gas and Water . Engineer.

There was a two hours' sitting of the Newton Urban District Council on Monday evening when the subject of the appointment of ass and water engineer caused a long discussion. In the unavoidable absence of Mr Collingwcod (chairman of the Council), the chair was occupied by Mr T Ormand. There werA also present : Messrs C B F Borron, J.P., J W Emmett, J.P., J Smith, Dr Patterson, Dr Latham, W Thompson, J Simpson, N Siddeley, F J Wood, W Gilmour, E L B ‘nt, El Evans, J Fitzgerald with C Cole (clerk), A Bowes (surveyor), aud 11 0 Timmins (gas and water engineer). 51R. SMITH AND THE APPOINTMENT OF GAS AND WATER ENGINEER.

Prior to proceeding with the consideration of the minutes of the several committees, Mr A Smith a3ked the Council to consider a motion which stood in his name and which read as follows .---"Tnat the resolution of the joint meeting of the Gas and Water Committee adopted by the Council at their meeting on the ath November last with reference- to the appointment of a water and gas engineer be rescinded, and that the ques- tion of appointment of a water engineer be referred to the Water Committee, and further that it be an instruc- tion to the Water Committee to consider how far the duties of water engineer can be performed by the mill- wright, proposed to be appointed, at all events until such time as the proposed eat msions of the waterworks shall be completed." Mr Smith contended that the Standing Orders allowed him to move that his motion should have precedence as it affected some of the minutes which would come before the Council for con- firmation. Ho theretore moved that his motion should have precedence. Mr E T Evans seconded. A discussion ensued in which Mr Gilmour, Me Bo rron, Dr Latham and the Chairman took part and eventually Mr Smith's proposition was carried by seven votes to six. Mr Smith then moved the motion which stood in his name and said that at the last meeting the Council resolved that a gas and water engineer be advertised for at a salary of £3OO per annum. Be was not prevent at that meeting, but, from what had transpired since, it seemed that that resalution was arrived at without sufficient regard for the whole of the circumstances of the case. At all events, the resolution did not commend itself to the Gas Committee for that committee had decided to appoint Mr Bowes to act as g 9 engineer. The Gas Committee had no desire tor a com- bined ;officer to manage the water department and the gas department. It seemed to him to follow that the resolution appointing a joint water and gas engineer must be rescinded. It would be useless to ap- point a joint water and gas engineer when he would not be allowed to have anything to do with the gas works, and it seemed to him that £3OO per annum was alto- gether too much to pay the gentleman to manage the Water Department alone. He wanted to remind the Council of what was being done with reference to the water extensions. They had appointed an expert firm to carry out their water extension scheme, and that firm would be responsible to the Council for the carrying out of that, and he contended that it would be a mistake for them to appoint an Engineer in addition. There were something like 300 applications for the position of mill-wright at the water works, and so far as those works were concerned the work was of a purely mechanical kind and a millwright would be able to do everything required until the extensions were handed over to them in a cemalete state. There was the financial aspect to be looked at. They were embarking upon expenditure which if not closely looked after must eventual's had to an increase of rates, and under the existing state of affairs they could not afford to pay £3OO per annum for an engineer of a Single department. They would have to pay the experts about £750 and £l2O for a millwright, and he thought It was altogether tco much to pay £3OO for a water engineer. Mr E T Evans seconded the motion, and thought the millwright would b 3 able to manage what was required ; at the present water works. It was necessary that the expenditure should b 3 kept" down or there would be an increase of rates. The Chairman pointed out that it was necessary to differentiate between rates and rentals. Any increased cost in the management of the Water Works would not cause an increase of rates, bat an increased water ren- tal which the consumer would have to pay. Mr Herron called attention to the fact that the Gas Committee was not only appointing a gas engineer in the person of Mr 133wes, but also a consulting Engineer to prepare an extension scheme and to advise the Com- mittee. He contended that if Mr Smith had been fully eisant of all the matters relating to the Water —.eat - would not have made the ob.eryations

RUGBY FOOTBALL,

By " Ovalist"

The two or three thousand spectators who assembled at Wilderspool on Saturday in the expectation of seeing good football were sadly disappointed. St. Helens and Warrington is usually an attractive bill of fare, but the match on Saturday between these two teams was con- spicuous by its unattractiveness. At St. Helens a few weeks before both sides played a good game which was won by Warrington, the score being 17 points to 9. Saturday ought to have furnished a repetition of the former contest, but for some reason or other it did not, excepting that Warrington won easily enough.

The more one expects from Warrington and the less one gets. Most people expected to see a smart display of back play by the senior league club, but the play fell far below that at St. Helens. At the commencement the popular anticipation seemed .justified, for Warring- ton attacked and by smart tactics at once took the lead, Fish running behind the posts from a splendid opening made by Dickenson. The scorer added a goal. Then Warrington appeared to ease off, the forwards playing a particularly ragged game, while the backs were erratic. Several good movements were spoiled, but before the interval Harris got hold and literally forced his oe ay through the St. Helens ranks to score far out. It was a tine effort and deserved the applause it gained. At half-time Warrington were leading by 8 points to nil.

St. Helens had played by no means badly, want of combination preventing them scoring more than once when energetic play had put them on the aggressive. The forwards played exceptionally well—in marked contrast to the Warrington eight, half of whom would do with putting on the " A" team for a bit to practice pushing and heeling out. They are playing far below form and not, in my opinion, doing their best.

In the second half St. Helens managed to take advan- tage of Warrington's laxity, Morrison scoring after Fish had feebly attempted to stop a rush. Later on a penalty goal was obtained. Warrington then went about their work more energetically, Dickenson once dodging clear away only to pass when he should have held on. At last Burgess broke through, and after exchanging with Isherwood he scored a good try, the goal-kick being a failure. Warrington's margin at the close was one of 11 points to 5 points.

I have said what a poor game it was, but I might add that there were plenty of spectators wl,o would not have been sorry to see the Saints vic- torious. Their efforts were certainly more deserving of reward than those of Warrington. Briers led the for- wards in many a fine effort,and in the rear Doherty and Foulkes both played in a style which shows that there is plenty of football left in them yet. The two halves, Morrison and Kennedy, played in lively fashion, but seemed short of experience. Burgess and Allen easily beat them, while among the home threequarters there was little to choose, all playing moderately. Fish was hampered a good deal with his injured hand, especially in tackling. Hallam was good, as usual, but allowed himself to be captured too often for the reputation of a reserve County full back.

In the county game at Stockport, Cheshire proved that their fine effort at Broughton was no fluke by easily knocking out the Yorkshiremen, the score reach- -13 points to 3 points in their favour at the close of a well-contested game. It was no secret that the Tykes meant to make up for want of class in the back division by the old, rushing style of forward play. The intention was excellent in theory but unsuccessful in practice. Cheshire possess a set of forwards afraid of nothing and they very soon showed their burly foes that they knew a thing or two themselves. Once checkmated in the front and Yorkshire's chances were at an end. The backs were outclassed by the aßuncorn-Stockport combination, Butterworth scoring twice and Robinson once, while Farmer placed a couple of goals. Rather Curiously Sam Houghton played below par and was quite overshadowed by Taylor of Hull, the custodian on the other side.

Lancashire and Cheshire- will stand on an equal footing in the County championship should the Palatin- ate repeat the dose Yorkshire have had to swallow. Presumably they will meet their neighbours again, rather than divide the honours. I would by no means lay odds on Lancashire. They were rather lucky at Broughton.

The Northern League game resulted in a further slaughter of the teams from over the border. Batley went down heavily at Leigh, Hull succumbed to Sal- ford, Brighouse to Swinton, while Hunslet had the worst of a game left unfinished fat Hunslet owing to darkness, Oldham being the visitors. The League table Up to date is as follows :---

The Oldham and Haaslet match 19 not it:win lad, a it wss left unfinished owing to darkness.

The Northern Union and Northern League meetings on Tuesday had important business before them. First and foremost was the deduction of 2 Points from Hunslet's score in the League for a breach of the pro- fessional laws. Todmorden and Hebden Bridge were similarly penalised in the Yorkshire Combination. A like charge against Oldham has resulted only in the " A " team being penalised, about which there will be some pretty loud grumbling in the Northern Lenue,the nature of the case notwithstanding. If any club deserved such a form of punishment it was Warring- ton, who were, instead, penalised in the premier league for an" A ' team player. Oldham have been lucky. so lucky that some awkward questions could and ought ! to be asked. -

The Oldham v Hunslet match has to be re-played, the gate being shared between the tNvo clubs and the Northern League in equal parts. Here again Olclham are lucky. Why should they share the gate ? It was their fault that Saturday's match was not begun 17 minutes earlier.

A full report of the business at these two meetings will be found on page 6.

Warrington could not ask for a harder nut to crack than they have in to-day's fixture with Batley at Batley-. The Cup-holders have already been badly beaten at Warrington, which to some extent makes up for the defeat in the final, and they are no doubt thirsting for revenge, especially as " Watty " Davies has undergone two months' suspension on account of his little fracas with Burgess and the Warrington crowd. Rather curi- ously Davies's suspension was up last Saturday, and to- day should be his first turn-out since he came under the ban of the Northern League Committee. If he plays there may be more than keenness displayed. Mr Bruckshaw, the appointed referee, will probably have his hands full, but he is a firm official and not a York• shireman, which is something to be thankful for.

The Warrington men are determined to make a big effort for victory, and they are doubtless encouraged by the severe thrashing the "gallant youths " got at Leigh last Saturday. A great deal will depend on the forwards, from whom a vastly improved display is ex- pected. Two of the regular eight have been dropped, viz., Cunningham and Eden, their places being taken by Walter Ashton and Edwards from the" team. The backs will be as usual, and if the team plays well I see no reason why they should not get another couple of points out of Batley.

The " A " team ought to give Latchford Rangers a warm time to-day judging by the talent at their dis- posal. Jones is back in town and turns out for the first time this season with Warrington, witile the backs 'Also include Knight, Hockenhall, Lawless, Ratcliffe, Townson and Duckworth.

A new forward will also be foußd on the " A " team to-day, in the person of Phillips, of Cathay Park, Cardiff, who has comes north with Jones. He is a big fellow, with the reputation of being a good scrimmager. I hope he turns out well.

It will be welcome news to many in Warrington to hear that the postponed match with Hull has been ar- ranged to take place on Shrove Tuesday, the 11th February next. After pancakes, therefore, those fortu- nate people who have a holiday on Shrove Tuesday will be able to assist their digestion by watching the "all blacks "pitted against the might of Warrington There is a prospective two points here, if the match a Huddersfield on the Saturday previous does not take too much out of the team.

lam not to be led into a discuss, of frivolous points. The broad point he evidently intended to lay down in his first letter concerning me was,that as I was a shareholder in the eminent firm of Fletcher, Russell and Co., Limited (whose quality of manufactures jus- tifies the purchase by the Corporation of their gas stoves) he was entitled, as a member of H Ingram, Limited, to claim that we stood in identical positions. I have replied to this point, but he again urges it and so it becomes again necessary for me to try and show him the comparative value of figures. Messrs Fletcher, Russell and Co., Limited, trading all over the world, have a capital of a quarter of a million of money, with hundreds of shareholders as Mr F R Roberts knows, and I hold less than & two hundred and fiftieth part of that nominal capital. Mr F R Roberts is a shareholder in H Ingram Limited,trad- ing in Warrington, with 13 shareaolders and a capital of less than £6OO, of which Mr F tt Roberts owns more than one tweltth so that for each £1 profit made by Messrs Fletcher, Russell and Co., Limited out of supply- ing the Corporation I should get less than one penny, whilst for each £l. profit made by H Ingram Limited, Mr F R Roberts would get more than ls 81. I have never claimed that it costs me £25 per year or any other sum, to become and remain a member of the Coancil of my native Borough. the town that has been so good to me I desire t serve, and think that whatever that service may cost me it is money well spent. Let me say that it is not Mr F R Roberts's "poor little £5O" in II laeram, Limited, that I object to, but it is any Company with a family prelomivance of share- holders having dual interests in ti-,e supply .ad c.onsurne. tion of goods required by the Corporation, aim I again repeat the reason—that it ix against public polmy. It is impossible to grt men of position, independence and means, who love their town well enough to give their services, without finding them with patriotism enough to support the industries of that town by their investmente. Mr F„R Roberts mast not confuse my notions with his own, and I am eurprised to find even him savieg " so that a member of the Corporation may have 9-10ths of the capital of a Company trading with the Corporation, but he must not be a director." Mr F R Roberta surely cannot think that there 13 any- thing so absurd in the minds of the thoughtful rate- payers. What they want, and are entitled to, is to be served by men who have a single eye to the public good, who have' clean hands, and who have the laudable desire to stand well with their fellow townsmen, in order that they may encourage that local patriotism and good feeling which can do 81 much to advance the pro- gress of their town and the comfort, convenience, and safety of i r, ratepayere.—Yours W. J. FORSHAW. 42, Sankey-street, November 29th.

The Legality of Municipal Tradin An Expert's Opinion. The opinion of Mr. Alex. Macm:rran, K C a recog- nised authority on municipal law, has teen taken in regard to the legality of varieus aspects of municipal trading, inchiding the position of a Councillor who may be not only shareholder, hut managing director of a limited Company trading with the C,rporation of which he is a member. Mr. Mtcnaorran's pronouncement is as follows : " A shareholder in a company which contracts With the Corporation would be disqualified under section 12 (11(c), but for tie provision in sub-sec- tion (2) (c) which provides that a person shall not 103 disqualified, or be deemed to have an interest in such a contract by reason only of his having a share OF interest in any company incorporated under the Comparues Act 1852. In other words if a firm contracted with the Corporation all the members cf it would be dirqualified to he councillors, but if a limited company contracted the shareholders would not be die qt edified." " The word ' niFy ' in 5.12 sub (2) is inserted to emphasize the provision, (which would ba implied without that while a perion is not to be dis- qualified by reason of his being a shareholder in a company contracting with the Council, that fact is not to he regarded as exempting him from dis- qualification on other grounds. Thus if a share- hN I ler were in receipt of a commission on the con- tract between the Company and the Council though he would not be disqualified .by reason of being a shareholder, yet he would have independently an interest in the contract which might disqualify him under sub-iection (1) (c)." " Toe bet that ia the present case the Councillor is managing director and in receipt of a salary from the Company, is, in my opinion, immaterial. Re has no interest in the contracts with the Council save as shareholder. If he were not a shareholder but simply a manager, sod in receipt of his present pay he would not be within sub-section (1)(c). It is , as shareholder only that he is within that clause,and it is as shareholder only that he is exempted by subsection (2) (e). In other words the section does
